\part*{\textcolor{utnavy}{Lecture 5: Balanced BSTs and AVL Trees}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{Lecture 5: Balanced BSTs and AVL Trees}
\setcounter{section}{0}

% ==================================================================
\section{Recall: the Problem with BSTs}

A plain BST implements the \textbf{Dictionary ADT} (Search, Insert, Delete), each in \textcolor{greenO}{$\Theta(h)$} where $h$ is the height. The catch: if we insert keys in sorted order $1, 2, 3, \ldots, n$, the tree becomes a \textcolor{rustW}{degenerate chain} with height $\Theta(n)$, so every operation is $\Theta(n)$.

\begin{keybox}[Goal]
Keep the tree \textbf{balanced} so its height stays \textcolor{greenO}{$\Theta(\log n)$}. Then Search, Insert, Delete are all \textcolor{greenO}{$\Theta(\log n)$}.
\end{keybox}

\textbf{Balanced BST} data structures for the Dictionary ADT: \textcolor{heapblue}{2-3 trees}, \textcolor{heapblue}{Red-Black trees}, \textcolor{heapblue}{B-Trees}, and \textcolor{rustW}{AVL trees} (this lecture).

% ==================================================================
\section{Height and Balance Factor}

\subsection{Height of a Node}

\begin{defbox}[Height]
$\text{height}(v)$ = number of \textcolor{rustW}{edges} on the longest path from $v$ down to a leaf.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item $\text{height}(v) = 0$ \quad if $v$ is a \textbf{leaf} (a single node).
  \item $\text{height}(\text{empty tree}) = -1$.
\end{itemize}
$\text{height}(T)$ = height of the root node of $T$.
\end{defbox}

The $-1$ for the empty tree is a convention that makes the recurrence $\text{height}(v) = 1 + \max(\text{height}(v.\text{left}),\, \text{height}(v.\text{right}))$ work even when a child is missing.

% ==================================================================
\subsection{Balance Factor}

\begin{defbox}[Balance Factor of a node $v$]
\begin{center}\large
$\text{BF}(v) \;=\; \text{height}(\text{right subtree of } v) \;-\; \text{height}(\text{left subtree of } v) \;=\; h_R - h_L$
\end{center}
\end{defbox}

The bigger $|\text{BF}(v)|$, the more \textcolor{rustW}{imbalanced} the node. We want every $\text{BF}(v)$ as close to $0$ as possible.

\begin{keybox}[Reading the Balance Factor]
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.4}
\begin{tabular}{c l}
$\text{BF}(v) = \textcolor{rustW}{+1}$ & $v$ is \textcolor{rustW}{\textbf{right-heavy}} \; ($h_R = h_L + 1$)\\
$\text{BF}(v) = \textcolor{greenO}{\phantom{+}0}$ & $v$ is \textcolor{greenO}{\textbf{balanced}} \; ($h_R = h_L$)\\
$\text{BF}(v) = \textcolor{heapblue}{-1}$ & $v$ is \textcolor{heapblue}{\textbf{left-heavy}} \; ($h_L = h_R + 1$)\\
\end{tabular}
\end{keybox}

% ==================================================================
\section{AVL Trees}

\subsection{Definition (Adelson-Velsky and Landis)}

\begin{defbox}[\textcolor{defblue}{AVL Tree (MUST KNOW)}]
An \textbf{AVL tree} $T$ is a BST where \textbf{every} node $v \in T$ satisfies
\begin{center}\Large
$-1 \;\;\textcolor{rustW}{\leq}\;\; \text{BF}(v) \;\;\textcolor{rustW}{\leq}\;\; +1$
\end{center}
Every node is left-heavy, balanced, or right-heavy. \textbf{No node may reach $\pm 2$.}
\end{defbox}

% ==================================================================
\subsection{Is This an AVL Tree?}

To check: compute $\text{BF}(v)$ at \emph{every} node. It is an AVL tree \textbf{iff} every $|\text{BF}(v)| \leq 1$. A single node with $|\text{BF}| \geq 2$ breaks it.

\begin{examplebox}[One tree, balance factor on every node]
Compute $h_R - h_L$ at each node (heights shown are of the subtree rooted there).

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
    level distance=1.15cm, sibling distance=2.0cm,
    every node/.style={circle, draw=heapblue, line width=0.7pt, minimum size=7mm, inner sep=1pt, font=\small, fill=white}
]
\node [label={[greenO,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]above right:{+1}}] {8}
    child {node [label={[heapblue,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]left:{-1}}] {4}
        child {node [label={[greenO,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]left:{0}}] {2}}
        child[missing]
    }
    child {node [label={[greenO,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]right:{+1}}] {12}
        child {node [label={[greenO,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]left:{0}}] {10}}
        child {node [label={[greenO,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]right:{+1}}] {14}
            child[missing]
            child {node [label={[greenO,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]right:{0}}] {15}}
        }
    };
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

Every $|\text{BF}| \leq 1$, so this \textbf{is} an AVL tree. \textcolor{greenO}{$\checkmark$}
\end{examplebox}

\begin{warnbox}[When it is NOT an AVL tree]
If any node reaches $\text{BF} = \textcolor{rustW}{+2}$ or $\text{BF} = \textcolor{rustW}{-2}$, the tree is not AVL. Example: a node whose left subtree has height $0$ and right subtree has height $2$ gives $\text{BF} = 2 - 0 = +2$. \textcolor{rustW}{Not AVL.}
\end{warnbox}

% ==================================================================
\subsection{Properties}

\begin{keybox}[Why AVL trees are good]
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=2pt]
  \item An AVL tree of $n$ nodes has height \textcolor{violetT}{$\Theta(\log n)$} \quad\big[precisely, $\text{height} \leq 1.44\,\log_2(n+2)$\big].
  \item Insert and Delete can maintain the balance in \textcolor{violetT}{$\Theta(\log n)$} time.
  \item Elegant, relatively clean and simple, works well in practice.
\end{itemize}
So Search, Insert, Delete are all \textcolor{greenO}{$\Theta(\log n)$} worst case.
\end{keybox}

\begin{exambox}[Seen on a past exam \textnormal{(Fall 2014 Midterm, Q3a)}]
\textbf{[4 marks]} Draw an AVL tree containing $\{1, 2, \ldots, 12\}$, but make it \textbf{as unbalanced as possible}. Next to each node, indicate the \textbf{height} of the subtree rooted at that node.

\begin{answerband}
``As unbalanced as possible'' = the \textbf{minimum} number of nodes are used to reach the height, so every node sits at its balance limit ($\text{BF} = \pm 1$). One valid answer (height shown as a subscript):

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
    level distance=1.0cm,
    every node/.style={circle, draw=heapblue, line width=0.7pt, minimum size=7mm, inner sep=1pt, font=\scriptsize, fill=white},
    level 1/.style={sibling distance=3.4cm},
    level 2/.style={sibling distance=1.8cm},
    level 3/.style={sibling distance=1.1cm}
]
\node {$8_{\,4}$}
  child {node {$5_{\,3}$}
    child {node {$3_{\,2}$}
      child {node {$2_{\,1}$}
        child {node {$1_{\,0}$}}
        child[missing]
      }
      child {node {$4_{\,0}$}}
    }
    child {node {$7_{\,1}$}
      child {node {$6_{\,0}$}}
      child[missing]
    }
  }
  child {node {$11_{\,2}$}
    child {node {$10_{\,1}$}
      child {node {$9_{\,0}$}}
      child[missing]
    }
    child {node {$12_{\,0}$}}
  };
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

\textcolor{violetT}{\textbf{Key idea:}} The subtree of height $4$ (root $8$) uses one child of height $3$ and, by the AVL rule, the other child may be as short as height $2$. That is the fewest nodes possible, which is why it looks so lopsided while still being AVL.
\end{answerband}
\end{exambox}

\begin{exambox}[Seen on a past exam \textnormal{(Fall 2014 Midterm, Q3c)}]
\textbf{[4 marks]} Let $m(h)$ = the \textbf{minimum} number of nodes in any AVL tree of height $h$. Give a recurrence for $m(h)$ with base cases, and explain it briefly.

\begin{answerband}
\[
  m(0) = 1, \qquad m(1) = 2, \qquad m(h) = 1 + m(h-1) + m(h-2) \;\; \text{for } h \geq 2.
\]
\textbf{Why:} height $0$ needs $1$ node; height $1$ needs a root with one child ($2$ nodes). For height $h$, the root ($1$ node) needs one child of height $h-1$; by the AVL condition the \emph{other} child can be as short as height $h-2$. Making both children minimal gives $m(h-1) + m(h-2)$ below the root.

\smallskip
\textcolor{violetT}{\textbf{Consequence:}} $m(h)$ grows like the Fibonacci numbers (exponentially in $h$), which forces $h = \Theta(\log n)$. This is the proof that AVL height is logarithmic.
\end{answerband}
\end{exambox}

% ==================================================================
\section{AVL Operations}

\begin{keybox}[The three operations]
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.4}
\begin{tabular}{l l}
\textcolor{heapblue}{\textbf{Search}$(T, x)$} & identical to BST search $\to$ \textcolor{greenO}{$O(\log n)$} (height is $O(\log n)$)\\
\textcolor{heapblue}{\textbf{Insert}$(T, x)$} & BST insert $+$ rebalance $\to$ \textcolor{violetT}{$O(\log n)$}\\
\textcolor{heapblue}{\textbf{Delete}$(T, x)$} & BST delete $+$ rebalance $\to$ \textcolor{violetT}{$O(\log n)$}\\
\end{tabular}
\end{keybox}

Search is unchanged from a plain BST, so the only new work is keeping the tree balanced after Insert (and Delete). This lecture covers \textbf{Insert}.

% ==================================================================
\section{Insert}

\subsection{General Idea}

\begin{keybox}[Insert$(T, x)$: the plan]
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.6em,itemsep=2pt]
  \item Insert $x$ into $T$ as in any BST. Now $x$ is a \textbf{leaf}; set $\text{BF}(x) = 0$.
  \item Walk \textbf{from $x$ up to the root}. Only the balance factors of nodes \emph{on this path} can change.
  \item At each node $v$ on the path, \textbf{adjust} $\text{BF}(v)$. If some $v$ becomes $\pm 2$, \textbf{rebalance} at $v$ with a rotation.
\end{enumerate}
\end{keybox}

\textbf{Why only the path changes:} inserting a leaf only lengthens paths that pass through $x$'s ancestors. No other node's subtree heights move.

\begin{keybox}[The stopping rule]
Going up, at each ancestor $v$:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item If the subtree containing $x$ got taller, $\text{BF}(v)$ moves by $\pm 1$.
  \item \textbf{If $\text{BF}(v)$ becomes $0$}, the subtree at $v$ did \emph{not} grow taller overall $\Rightarrow$ \textcolor{greenO}{stop}. Nothing above $v$ can change.
  \item If $\text{BF}(v)$ becomes $\pm 2$, rotate to fix it (then the height is restored, so we also stop).
\end{itemize}
\end{keybox}

% ==================================================================
\subsection{Worked Example 1: No Rebalancing}

Start from this AVL tree of $\{1, 3, 7, 12, 14, 17, 19\}$ (all $\text{BF} = 0$), and \textbf{Insert 8}.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
    level distance=1.1cm, sibling distance=2.2cm,
    every node/.style={circle, draw=heapblue, line width=0.7pt, minimum size=7mm, inner sep=1pt, font=\small, fill=white},
    level 2/.style={sibling distance=1.2cm}
]
\node [label={[greyline,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]above right:{$0\to-1$}}] {12}
    child {node [label={[greyline,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]left:{$0\to+1$}}] {3}
        child {node [label={[greyline,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]left:{0}}] {1}}
        child {node [label={[rustW,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]right:{$0\to+1$}}] {7}
            child[missing]
            child {node[fill=greenO!18,draw=greenO] [label={[greenO,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]right:{0}}] {8}}
        }
    }
    child {node [label={[greyline,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]right:{0}}] {17}
        child {node {14}}
        child {node {19}}
    };
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

\begin{examplebox}[Trace: Insert 8]
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=2pt]
  \item BST-insert $8$: it lands as the \textbf{right child of 7}. Set $\text{BF}(8) = 0$.
  \item Go up to $7$: $8$ is in $7$'s right subtree $\Rightarrow$ $\text{BF}(7): 0 \to +1$. Still legal, and \textbf{the height of the subtree at 7 did not change} (it was $1$ before, it is still $1$). \textcolor{rustW}{So we stop at 7.}
\end{enumerate}
\textcolor{violetT}{\textbf{Because the height of 7's subtree is unchanged, no ancestor above it is affected}} (3 and 12 keep their old balance factors, shown greyed). \textcolor{greenO}{No rebalancing needed.}
\end{examplebox}

\begin{warnbox}[Watch the ``+1 at 3'' question]
After inserting $8$, node $3$ would only change if $7$'s subtree grew. It did not, so we never even reach $3$: we stop the moment a node keeps its height. This is the whole point of the stopping rule.
\end{warnbox}

% ==================================================================
\subsection{Worked Example 2: Single Rotation}

Now the tree is the AVL of $\{1, 3, 5, 7, 8, 12, 14, 17, 19\}$. \textbf{Insert 10.}

$10$ BST-inserts as the \textbf{right child of 8}. Walking up: $\text{BF}(8): 0 \to +1$, then $\text{BF}(7): 0 \to +1$, then $\text{BF}(3): +1 \to \textcolor{rustW}{+2}$. \textcolor{rustW}{Node 3 is now unbalanced (right-heavy), and its right child 7 is also right-heavy ($+1$).}

\begin{keybox}[This is the ``right-right'' (RR) case $\Rightarrow$ single Left Rotation]
$v = 3$ has $\text{BF}(v) = +2$ \textbf{and} $\text{BF}(v.\text{right}) = +1$. Fix with one \textbf{Left Rotation} at $v$.
\end{keybox}

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
    every node/.style={circle, draw=heapblue, line width=0.7pt, minimum size=7mm, inner sep=1pt, font=\small, fill=white}
]
% ---- BEFORE (left) ----
\node[font=\bfseries, draw=none, fill=none] at (0.3,0.9) {Before:};
\node[draw=rustW,line width=1.1pt, label={[rustW,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]left:{+2}}] (b3) at (0.6,0) {3};
\node[label={[greyline,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]left:{0}}] (b1) at (-0.6,-1.2) {1};
\node[draw=rustW,line width=1.1pt, label={[rustW,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]right:{+1}}] (b7) at (1.8,-1.2) {7};
\node[label={[greyline,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]left:{0}}] (b5) at (0.9,-2.4) {5};
\node[label={[rustW,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]right:{+1}}] (b8) at (2.7,-2.4) {8};
\node (b10) at (3.4,-3.6) {10};
\draw (b3)--(b1); \draw (b3)--(b7); \draw (b7)--(b5); \draw (b7)--(b8); \draw (b8)--(b10);

% ---- arrow ----
\draw[-{Stealth}, line width=1.2pt, rustW] (4.3,-1.8) -- (6.0,-1.8);
\node[rustW,font=\small\bfseries, draw=none, fill=none] at (5.15,-1.45) {Left Rotation};

% ---- AFTER (right) ----
\node[font=\bfseries, draw=none, fill=none] at (8.0,0.9) {After:};
\node[draw=rustW,line width=1.1pt, label={[greenO,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]above right:{0}}] (a7) at (8.4,0) {7};
\node[draw=rustW,line width=1.1pt, label={[greenO,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]left:{0}}] (a3) at (7.4,-1.2) {3};
\node[label={[rustW,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]right:{+1}}] (a8) at (9.4,-1.2) {8};
\node (a1) at (6.7,-2.4) {1};
\node[fill=goldpath!25,draw=goldpath] (a5) at (8.1,-2.4) {5};
\node (a10) at (10.1,-2.4) {10};
\draw (a7)--(a3); \draw (a7)--(a8); \draw (a3)--(a1); \draw (a3)--(a5); \draw (a8)--(a10);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

\begin{examplebox}[Reading the rotation]
Left-rotating at $3$: its right child $7$ becomes the new subtree root, and $3$ becomes $7$'s \textbf{left} child.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=2pt]
  \item \textbf{Where does 5 go?} $5$ was $7$'s \emph{left} subtree. In a left rotation, $7$'s old left subtree becomes $3$'s new \textbf{right} subtree. So \textcolor{goldpath}{$5$ hangs under $3$, not under $8$}, because $3 < 5 < 7$, so $5$ belongs on $3$'s right. (BST property preserved.)
  \item After: $\text{BF}(7) = 0$, so $7$'s subtree is balanced again.
\end{itemize}
\end{examplebox}

\begin{keybox}[Bonus: nothing propagates upward]
The subtree rooted at $3$ had height $2$ \emph{before} inserting $10$; after the rotation the subtree rooted at $7$ has height $2$ again. \textbf{Same height as before the insert}, so the balance factor of $12$ (the parent) is unchanged and we \textcolor{greenO}{stop}. One rotation fixes everything.
\end{keybox}

% ==================================================================
\subsection{Worked Example 3: Double Rotation}

Tree is the AVL of $\{1, 3, 5, 7, 8, 12, 14, 17, 19\}$. \textbf{Insert 6.}

$6$ BST-inserts as the \textbf{right child of 5}. Walking up: $\text{BF}(5): 0 \to +1$, $\text{BF}(7): 0 \to \textcolor{heapblue}{-1}$, $\text{BF}(3): +1 \to \textcolor{rustW}{+2}$. Now $v = 3$ has $\text{BF} = +2$, but its right child $7$ is \textcolor{heapblue}{left-heavy ($-1$)}: the ``bend'' zig-zags.

\begin{warnbox}[A single Left Rotation does NOT fix this]
Because the heavy grandchild is on the \emph{left} of the right child (a right-then-left zig-zag), one left rotation at $3$ leaves the tree unbalanced. We need \textbf{two} rotations.
\end{warnbox}

\begin{keybox}[This is the ``right-left'' (RL) case $\Rightarrow$ Double Right-Left Rotation]
$v = 3$ has $\text{BF}(v) = +2$ \textbf{and} $\text{BF}(v.\text{right}) = -1$.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.6em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item \textbf{Right-rotate} the right subtree of $v$ (rotate around $7$).
  \item \textbf{Left-rotate} at $v$ (rotate around $3$).
\end{enumerate}
\end{keybox}

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
    every node/.style={circle, draw=heapblue, line width=0.7pt, minimum size=6.5mm, inner sep=1pt, font=\scriptsize, fill=white}
]
% ---- START ----
\node[font=\bfseries\scriptsize, draw=none, fill=none] at (0.0,0.9) {Insert 6:};
\node[draw=rustW,line width=1pt, label={[rustW,font=\tiny\bfseries]left:{+2}}] (s3) at (0.4,0) {3};
\node[label={[greyline,font=\tiny\bfseries]left:{0}}] (s1) at (-0.5,-1.1) {1};
\node[draw=rustW,line width=1pt, label={[heapblue,font=\tiny\bfseries]right:{-1}}] (s7) at (1.3,-1.1) {7};
\node[label={[rustW,font=\tiny\bfseries]left:{+1}}] (s5) at (0.5,-2.2) {5};
\node[label={[greyline,font=\tiny\bfseries]right:{0}}] (s8) at (2.1,-2.2) {8};
\node[fill=greenO!18,draw=greenO] (s6) at (1.1,-3.3) {6};
\draw (s3)--(s1); \draw (s3)--(s7); \draw (s7)--(s5); \draw (s7)--(s8); \draw (s5)--(s6);

% arrow 1
\draw[-{Stealth}, line width=1pt, rustW] (2.7,-1.6) -- (3.7,-1.6);
\node[rustW,font=\tiny\bfseries, draw=none, fill=none, align=center] at (3.2,-1.15) {Step 1:\\ right-rot 7};

% ---- MIDDLE (after right-rotate around 7) ----
\node[draw=rustW,line width=1pt, label={[rustW,font=\tiny\bfseries]left:{+2}}] (m3) at (4.4,0) {3};
\node[label={[greyline,font=\tiny\bfseries]left:{0}}] (m1) at (3.5,-1.1) {1};
\node[label={[rustW,font=\tiny\bfseries]right:{+1}}] (m5) at (5.3,-1.1) {5};
\node[label={[greyline,font=\tiny\bfseries]right:{+1}}] (m7) at (6.1,-2.2) {7};
\node (m6) at (5.3,-3.3) {6};
\node (m8) at (6.9,-3.3) {8};
\draw (m3)--(m1); \draw (m3)--(m5); \draw (m5)--(m7); \draw (m7)--(m6); \draw (m7)--(m8);

% arrow 2
\draw[-{Stealth}, line width=1pt, rustW] (7.4,-1.6) -- (8.4,-1.6);
\node[rustW,font=\tiny\bfseries, draw=none, fill=none, align=center] at (7.9,-1.15) {Step 2:\\ left-rot 3};

% ---- END (after left-rotate around 3) ----
\node[label={[greenO,font=\tiny\bfseries]above right:{0}}] (e5) at (9.9,0) {5};
\node[label={[greenO,font=\tiny\bfseries]left:{0}}] (e3) at (9.0,-1.1) {3};
\node[label={[greenO,font=\tiny\bfseries]right:{0}}] (e7) at (10.8,-1.1) {7};
\node (e1) at (8.4,-2.2) {1};
\node (e6) at (10.2,-2.2) {6};
\node (e8) at (11.4,-2.2) {8};
\draw (e5)--(e3); \draw (e5)--(e7); \draw (e3)--(e1); \draw (e7)--(e6); \draw (e7)--(e8);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

\begin{examplebox}[Result of the double rotation]
The middle key $5$ rises to the top of the subtree, with $3$ on its left and $7$ on its right. Every node is back to $\text{BF} = 0$, the tree is still a BST, and the subtree height matches what it was \textbf{before} inserting $6$, so we \textcolor{greenO}{stop}: no rebalancing propagates to $12$.
\end{examplebox}

% ==================================================================
\subsection{The Four Rotation Cases}

At the lowest unbalanced node $v$ (with $\text{BF}(v) = \pm 2$), which fix to apply is decided by the balance factor of the child on the heavy side.

\begin{keybox}[Rotation case table]
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.55}
\begin{tabular}{c c l l}
\textbf{$\text{BF}(v)$} & \textbf{heavy child's BF} & \textbf{Shape} & \textbf{Fix}\\
\hline
$+2$ & $\text{BF}(v.\text{right}) = +1$ & right-right (RR) & \textcolor{rustW}{single Left rotation at $v$}\\
$+2$ & $\text{BF}(v.\text{right}) = -1$ & right-left (RL) & \textcolor{rustW}{Right rotation on $v.\text{right}$, then Left at $v$}\\
$-2$ & $\text{BF}(v.\text{left}) = -1$ & left-left (LL) & \textcolor{rustW}{single Right rotation at $v$}\\
$-2$ & $\text{BF}(v.\text{left}) = +1$ & left-right (LR) & \textcolor{rustW}{Left rotation on $v.\text{left}$, then Right at $v$}\\
\end{tabular}
\end{keybox}

\begin{keybox}[Rule of thumb]
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item Heavy side and grandchild's heavy side \textbf{agree} (both right, or both left) $\Rightarrow$ \textcolor{rustW}{single} rotation.
  \item They \textbf{disagree} (a zig-zag) $\Rightarrow$ \textcolor{rustW}{double} rotation (fix the child first, then $v$).
\end{itemize}
After the rotation the subtree's height returns to what it was before the insert, so \textbf{at most one rotation (single or double) is ever needed per insert}.
\end{keybox}

% ==================================================================
\subsection{Insert Algorithm Sketch}

\begin{codebox}[Insert(T, x)]
\ttfamily 1. Insert x into T as in any BST.\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{1.5em}x is now a leaf; set BF(x) = 0.\\[3pt]
\ttfamily 2. Go up from x to the root. For each node v on this path:\\[2pt]
\ttfamily \hspace*{1.5em}Adjust BF(v):\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{3em}if x is in the RIGHT subtree of v:\, increment BF(v)\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{3em}if x is in the LEFT  subtree of v:\, decrement BF(v)\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{3em}if BF(v) == 0:\, \textnormal{\itshape (subtree height unchanged)} \, STOP\\[3pt]
\ttfamily \hspace*{1.5em}Rebalance if necessary:\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{3em}if BF(v) == +2:\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{5em}if BF(v.right) == +1:\, Left rotation; update BFs; STOP\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{5em}if BF(v.right) == -1:\, Right-Left rotation; update BFs; STOP\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{3em}if BF(v) == -2:\, \textnormal{\itshape (symmetric: Right or Left-Right rotation)} STOP
\end{codebox}

\textbf{Cost:} the walk up is at most the height $= O(\log n)$, each step is $O(1)$, and at most one rotation ($O(1)$) happens. So \textbf{Insert is $\textcolor{violetT}{O(\log n)}$}.

% ==================================================================
% ==================================================================
\section{Midterm-Style Process Problems}

\begin{examplebox}[Predicted: AVL Delete with rebalancing]
Delete $2$ from this AVL tree, then restore the AVL property.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
   level distance=1.05cm,
   every node/.style={circle, draw=heapblue, line width=0.7pt, minimum size=7mm, inner sep=1pt, font=\small, fill=white},
   level 1/.style={sibling distance=2.8cm}, level 2/.style={sibling distance=1.5cm}, level 3/.style={sibling distance=1.1cm}
]
\node [label={[greyline,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]above left:{$+1$}}] {5}
  child {node [label={[greyline,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]left:{$-1$}}] {3}
    child {node[fill=rustW!15,draw=rustW] {2}} child[missing] }
  child {node [label={[greyline,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]right:{$+1$}}] {8}
    child {node {7}}
    child {node [label={[greyline,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]right:{$+1$}}] {9} child[missing] child {node {10}} } };
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

\begin{answerband}
\textbf{Delete 2} (a leaf): the left subtree of $5$ shrinks from height $1$ to $0$. Walk up: at $5$, $\text{BF}=2-0=\textcolor{rustW}{+2}$ and its right child $8$ is right-heavy ($+1$): \textbf{RR}, so a single \textbf{Left rotation at 5}.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[every node/.style={circle, draw=heapblue, line width=0.7pt, minimum size=7mm, inner sep=1pt, font=\small, fill=white}]
\node[font=\bfseries\scriptsize, draw=none, fill=none] at (0.0,0.8) {After delete 2:};
\node[draw=rustW,line width=1.1pt,label={[rustW,font=\tiny\bfseries]left:{$+2$}}] (f5) at (0.8,0) {5};
\node (f3) at (-0.3,-1.1) {3};
\node[draw=rustW,line width=1.1pt,label={[rustW,font=\tiny\bfseries]right:{$+1$}}] (f8) at (1.9,-1.1) {8};
\node (f7) at (1.2,-2.2) {7};
\node[label={[greyline,font=\tiny\bfseries]right:{$+1$}}] (f9) at (2.6,-2.2) {9};
\node (f10) at (3.2,-3.3) {10};
\draw (f5)--(f3); \draw (f5)--(f8); \draw (f8)--(f7); \draw (f8)--(f9); \draw (f9)--(f10);
\draw[-{Stealth}, line width=1.2pt, rustW] (4.0,-1.6) -- (5.5,-1.6);
\node[rustW,font=\scriptsize\bfseries, draw=none, fill=none] at (4.75,-1.25) {Left-rot 5};
\node[font=\bfseries\scriptsize, draw=none, fill=none] at (6.2,0.8) {Balanced:};
\node[label={[greenO,font=\tiny\bfseries]above right:{$0$}}] (g8) at (7.6,0) {8};
\node[label={[greenO,font=\tiny\bfseries]left:{$0$}}] (g5) at (6.6,-1.1) {5};
\node[label={[greyline,font=\tiny\bfseries]right:{$+1$}}] (g9) at (8.6,-1.1) {9};
\node (g3) at (5.9,-2.2) {3};
\node[fill=goldpath!25,draw=goldpath] (g7) at (7.3,-2.2) {7};
\node (g10) at (9.3,-2.2) {10};
\draw (g8)--(g5); \draw (g8)--(g9); \draw (g5)--(g3); \draw (g5)--(g7); \draw (g9)--(g10);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}
$8$ rises, and its old left child $7$ moves to become the \textcolor{goldpath}{right child of $5$} ($5<7<8$). Remember: unlike Insert, a Delete rotation can leave the subtree shorter, so you may need to keep rebalancing up toward the root.
\end{answerband}
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}[Predicted: build an AVL by inserting a sequence]
Insert $30$, then $10$, then $20$ into an empty AVL tree. Show the rebalancing.

\begin{answerband}
$30$ then $10$ gives a legal tree ($30$ is left-heavy, $\text{BF}=-1$). Inserting $20$ goes $30\to10\to$ right, making $\text{BF}(10)=+1$ and $\text{BF}(30)=\textcolor{rustW}{-2}$ with left child $10$ \emph{right-heavy}: the \textbf{LR} zig-zag.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[every node/.style={circle, draw=heapblue, line width=0.7pt, minimum size=6.5mm, inner sep=1pt, font=\scriptsize, fill=white}]
% start
\node[draw=rustW,line width=1pt,label={[rustW,font=\tiny\bfseries]right:{$-2$}}] (s30) at (0.6,0) {30};
\node[label={[rustW,font=\tiny\bfseries]left:{$+1$}}] (s10) at (-0.2,-1.1) {10};
\node (s20) at (0.6,-2.2) {20};
\draw (s30)--(s10); \draw (s10)--(s20);
\draw[-{Stealth},line width=1pt,rustW] (1.4,-1.1)--(2.4,-1.1);
\node[rustW,font=\tiny\bfseries,draw=none,fill=none,align=center] at (1.9,-0.7){left-rot 10,\\ right-rot 30};
% end
\node[label={[greenO,font=\tiny\bfseries]above:{$0$}}] (e20) at (3.6,0) {20};
\node (e10) at (2.9,-1.1) {10};
\node (e30) at (4.3,-1.1) {30};
\draw (e20)--(e10); \draw (e20)--(e30);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}
The middle key $20$ rises to the root with $10$ left and $30$ right. One double rotation fixes it, and at most one rotation (single or double) is ever needed per insert.
\end{answerband}
\end{examplebox}

\section*{\textcolor{violetT}{Quick Reference: Lecture 5}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quick Reference: Lecture 5}
\begin{keybox}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.4}
\begin{tabular}{l l}
Height & edges to deepest leaf; leaf $=0$, empty $=-1$\\
$\text{BF}(v)$ & $h_R - h_L$; \; $+1$ right-heavy, $0$ balanced, $-1$ left-heavy\\
AVL tree & BST with $-1 \leq \text{BF}(v) \leq +1$ for \emph{every} node\\
AVL height & $\Theta(\log n)$ \; ($\leq 1.44\log_2(n+2)$); \, $m(h)=1+m(h{-}1)+m(h{-}2)$\\
Search & same as BST $\to$ $O(\log n)$\\
Insert & BST-insert leaf, walk up adjusting BF, rotate if $\pm2$ $\to$ $O(\log n)$\\
Stop rule & stop when a node's BF becomes $0$ (its height did not change)\\
RR ($+2,+1$) & single Left rotation\\
LL ($-2,-1$) & single Right rotation\\
RL ($+2,-1$) & Right rotation on child, then Left at $v$\\
LR ($-2,+1$) & Left rotation on child, then Right at $v$\\
Rotations/insert & at most one (single or double)\\
\end{tabular}
\end{keybox}

\vspace{4pt}
\begin{center}\textcolor{greyline}{\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.6pt}}\end{center}
\newpage

% ******************************************************************
%  PART VI: LECTURE 6 — AVL TREES (PART 2): WHY INSERT WORKS
% ******************************************************************

\part*{\textcolor{utnavy}{Lecture 6: AVL Insert Correctness and Cost}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{Lecture 6: AVL Insert Correctness and Cost}
\setcounter{section}{0}

% ==================================================================
\section{What This Lecture Adds}

Lecture 5 gave the \textbf{Insert} procedure by example and the \textbf{four rotation cases} (RR, LL, RL, LR). This lecture does \emph{not} re-teach those. It proves the procedure restores the AVL property in \textbf{every} case, states the \textbf{node layout}, and shows each rotation is \textcolor{greenO}{$\Theta(1)$}.

\begin{keybox}[Setup: the first unbalanced node]
Insert $x$ as a BST leaf, then walk up. Let \textcolor{rustW}{$A$} = the \textbf{first} node on the path from $x$ to the root with $|\text{BF}(A)| = 2$. Everything strictly below $A$ is still AVL.

By symmetry assume $\text{BF}(A) = \textcolor{rustW}{+2}$ (the $-2$ case is a mirror image). Then before the insert $\text{BF}(A) = +1$, and the insert went into $A$'s \textbf{right} subtree and \textbf{increased its height} from $h$ to $h+1$. $A$'s left subtree still has height $h$.
\end{keybox}

Let \textcolor{heapblue}{$B$} = right child of $A$ (root of the subtree that grew to height $h+1$).

% ==================================================================
\section{Why the Insert Algorithm is Correct}

\subsection{The Skipped Step: $\text{BF}(B) \neq 0$}

Before rotating we must know $B$'s shape. Since $A$ is the \textbf{first} unbalanced node, $B$ is still balanced: $\text{BF}(B) \in \{-1, 0, +1\}$.

\begin{keybox}[Claim: $\text{BF}(B)$ cannot be $0$]
So $\text{BF}(B) = +1$ (\textbf{Case 1a}) or $\text{BF}(B) = -1$ (\textbf{Case 1b}). These are the only two cases.
\end{keybox}

\textbf{Why $\text{BF}(B) = 0$ is impossible.} The insert of a single leaf increased $B$'s height by exactly $1$ (from $h$ to $h+1$). One leaf can lengthen only \textbf{one} of $B$'s two subtrees. If $\text{BF}(B) = 0$ now (both subtrees height $h$), then before the insert one subtree was height $h$ already, so $B$ had height $h+1$ \emph{before} the insert too. That contradicts ``$B$'s height increased.'' \hfill $\square$

\begin{warnbox}[Reading the two cases]
$\text{BF}(B) = \textcolor{rustW}{+1}$: the growth is on $B$'s \textbf{right} (right-right, RR).\\
$\text{BF}(B) = \textcolor{heapblue}{-1}$: the growth is on $B$'s \textbf{left} (right-left, RL). Here name $C = B.\text{left}$.
\end{warnbox}

% ==================================================================
\subsection{Case 1(a): Single Left Rotation}

$x$ landed in $B$'s right subtree. Fix with a single \textbf{Left Rotation at $A$}.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  every node/.style={circle, draw=heapblue, line width=0.7pt, minimum size=7mm, inner sep=1pt, font=\small, fill=white},
  tri/.style={regular polygon, regular polygon sides=3, draw=greyline, fill=skyfill, minimum size=9mm, inner sep=0pt, font=\scriptsize, shape border rotate=0}
]
% BEFORE
\node[font=\bfseries, draw=none, fill=none] at (0.2,0.9) {Before:};
\node[draw=rustW,line width=1.1pt,label={[rustW,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]left:{$+2$}}] (A) at (0.8,0) {$A$};
\node[draw=heapblue,label={[rustW,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]right:{$+1$}}] (B) at (2.1,-1.2) {$B$};
\node[tri] (T1) at (-0.3,-1.4) {$T_1$};
\node[tri] (T2) at (1.3,-2.4) {$T_2$};
\node[tri] (T3) at (2.9,-2.4) {$T_3$};
\draw (A)--(B); \draw (A)--(T1); \draw (B)--(T2); \draw (B)--(T3);
\node[draw=none,fill=none,font=\scriptsize\itshape,text=greyline] at (-0.3,-2.1) {$h$};
\node[draw=none,fill=none,font=\scriptsize\itshape,text=greyline] at (1.3,-3.05) {$h$};
\node[draw=none,fill=none,font=\scriptsize\itshape,text=greyline] at (2.9,-3.05) {$h{+}1$};
% arrow
\draw[-{Stealth},line width=1.2pt,rustW] (4.0,-1.4)--(5.6,-1.4);
\node[rustW,font=\small\bfseries,draw=none,fill=none] at (4.8,-1.05) {Left Rotate};
% AFTER
\node[font=\bfseries,draw=none,fill=none] at (7.4,0.9) {After:};
\node[draw=heapblue,label={[greenO,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]above right:{$0$}}] (B2) at (8.2,0) {$B$};
\node[draw=heapblue,label={[greenO,font=\scriptsize\bfseries]left:{$0$}}] (A2) at (7.2,-1.2) {$A$};
\node[tri] (t3) at (9.2,-1.4) {$T_3$};
\node[tri] (t1) at (6.4,-2.4) {$T_1$};
\node[tri] (t2) at (8.0,-2.4) {$T_2$};
\draw (B2)--(A2); \draw (B2)--(t3); \draw (A2)--(t1); \draw (A2)--(t2);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

\begin{examplebox}[What the rotation does]
$B$ rises, $A$ becomes $B$'s left child, and $B$'s old left subtree $T_2$ (height $h$) becomes $A$'s new right subtree. Order $T_1\, A\, T_2\, B\, T_3$ is unchanged, so the \textbf{BST property holds}. New factors: $\text{BF}(A)=0$, $\text{BF}(B)=0$.
\end{examplebox}

\begin{keybox}[Bonus: height is restored]
Before inserting $x$, the subtree at $A$ had height $h+2$. After the rotation it is $h+2$ again. \textbf{Same height as before the insert} $\Rightarrow$ no ancestor's BF changes $\Rightarrow$ \textcolor{greenO}{stop}. One rotation finishes the whole insert.
\end{keybox}

% ==================================================================
\subsection{Case 1(b): Double Right-Left Rotation}

$x$ landed in $B$'s \textbf{left} subtree, rooted at $C = B.\text{left}$. A single left rotation would \textbf{not} fix it. Do two: \textbf{right-rotate at $B$}, then \textbf{left-rotate at $A$}.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  every node/.style={circle, draw=heapblue, line width=0.7pt, minimum size=6.5mm, inner sep=1pt, font=\scriptsize, fill=white},
  tri/.style={regular polygon, regular polygon sides=3, draw=greyline, fill=skyfill, minimum size=7mm, inner sep=0pt, font=\tiny}
]
% BEFORE
\node[font=\bfseries\scriptsize,draw=none,fill=none] at (0.0,0.9) {Before:};
\node[draw=rustW,line width=1pt,label={[rustW,font=\tiny\bfseries]left:{$+2$}}] (A) at (0.7,0) {$A$};
\node[draw=heapblue,label={[heapblue,font=\tiny\bfseries]right:{$-1$}}] (B) at (2.0,-1.1) {$B$};
\node[draw=heapblue] (C) at (1.1,-2.2) {$C$};
\node[tri] (T1) at (-0.3,-1.3) {$T_1$};
\node[tri] (T3) at (2.9,-2.2) {$T_3$};
\node[tri] (T21) at (0.4,-3.3) {$T_{21}$};
\node[tri] (T22) at (1.8,-3.3) {$T_{22}$};
\draw (A)--(B); \draw (A)--(T1); \draw (B)--(C); \draw (B)--(T3); \draw (C)--(T21); \draw (C)--(T22);
% arrow
\draw[-{Stealth},line width=1pt,rustW] (3.7,-1.6)--(4.7,-1.6);
\node[rustW,font=\tiny\bfseries,draw=none,fill=none,align=center] at (4.2,-1.15) {2 rotations};
% AFTER
\node[font=\bfseries\scriptsize,draw=none,fill=none] at (5.6,0.9) {After:};
\node[draw=greenO,line width=1pt,label={[greenO,font=\tiny\bfseries]above:{$0$}}] (C2) at (7.4,0) {$C$};
\node[draw=heapblue,label={[greenO,font=\tiny\bfseries]left:{$0/{-}1$}}] (A2) at (6.3,-1.2) {$A$};
\node[draw=heapblue,label={[greenO,font=\tiny\bfseries]right:{${+}1/0$}}] (B2) at (8.5,-1.2) {$B$};
\node[tri] (t1) at (5.5,-2.4) {$T_1$};
\node[tri] (t21) at (7.1,-2.4) {$T_{21}$};
\node[tri] (t22) at (7.9,-2.4) {$T_{22}$};
\node[tri] (t3) at (9.3,-2.4) {$T_3$};
\draw (C2)--(A2); \draw (C2)--(B2); \draw (A2)--(t1); \draw (A2)--(t21); \draw (B2)--(t22); \draw (B2)--(t3);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

\begin{examplebox}[What the double rotation does]
$C$ (the middle key) rises to the top. Its two subtrees $T_{21}, T_{22}$ split: $T_{21}$ goes under $A$, $T_{22}$ goes under $B$. Order $T_1\, A\, T_{21}\, C\, T_{22}\, B\, T_3$ is preserved, so the \textbf{BST property holds}. Final factors: $\text{BF}(C)=0$; $A$ and $B$ get $\{0, -1\}$ and $\{+1, 0\}$ depending on which of $T_{21}, T_{22}$ received $x$. The subtree height returns to $h+2$, so again we \textcolor{greenO}{stop}.
\end{examplebox}

\begin{keybox}[Special case $h = -1$]
When $h=-1$, the subtrees are empty and $C$ \textbf{is} the inserted leaf $x$. The double rotation collapses to: $A$, $x$, $B$ become root $x$ with children $A$ and $B$, all $\text{BF}=0$. Same rule, no special handling needed.
\end{keybox}

% ==================================================================
\section{Cost, Node Layout, and Algorithm}

\subsection{Rotation Cost and Total Insert Cost}

\begin{keybox}[A rotation is $\Theta(1)$]
A left (or right) rotation rewires only \textbf{3 pairs of pointers}. No subtree is copied or traversed $\Rightarrow$ \textcolor{greenO}{$\Theta(1)$}.
\end{keybox}

\begin{keybox}[Insert is $\Theta(\log n)$]
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.3}
\begin{tabular}{l l}
BST insert of the leaf & $O(\log n)$ \\
Walk up adjusting BF & $O(\log n)$, \; $O(1)$ per node \\
Rebalance & \textbf{at most one} rotation (single or double), $\Theta(1)$ \\
\end{tabular}

The ``height restored'' bonus is what caps it at \textbf{one} rotation: after rotating, the subtree has its pre-insert height, so nothing above changes. Total: \textcolor{violetT}{$O(\log n)$}.
\end{keybox}

% ==================================================================
\subsection{Contents of an AVL Node}

\begin{defbox}[AVL node layout]
Each node stores \textbf{5 fields}:
\begin{center}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.35}
\begin{tabular}{l l}
\hline
\textcolor{rustW}{\textbf{PARENT}} & pointer to parent (needed to walk up)\\
\texttt{key} & the element's key (data)\\
\texttt{BF} & balance factor $\in \{-1,0,+1\}$ (stored, not recomputed)\\
\textcolor{greenO}{\textbf{LEFT CHILD}} & pointer to left child\\
\textcolor{heapblue}{\textbf{RIGHT CHILD}} & pointer to right child\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Storing \texttt{BF} in the node lets the walk-up update it in $O(1)$ per step instead of recomputing subtree heights.
\end{defbox}

% ==================================================================
\subsection{Final Insert Algorithm}

\begin{codebox}[Insert(T, x)]
\ttfamily Insert x into T as in any BST; x is now a leaf; set BF(x) = 0.\\[3pt]
\ttfamily Go up from x to the root. For each node v on the path:\\[2pt]
\ttfamily \hspace*{1.5em}Adjust BF(v):\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{3em}if x is in RIGHT subtree of v:\, BF(v) $\mathbin{+}{=}$ 1\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{3em}if x is in LEFT  subtree of v:\, BF(v) $\mathbin{-}{=}$ 1\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{3em}if BF(v) == 0:\, STOP \hfill\textnormal{\itshape (height unchanged)}\\[3pt]
\ttfamily \hspace*{1.5em}Rebalance:\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{3em}if BF(v) == +2:\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{5em}if BF(v.right)==+1:\, Left Rotation; update BFs; STOP\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{5em}if BF(v.right)==$-$1:\, Right-Left Rotation; update BFs; STOP\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{3em}if BF(v) == $-$2:\, \textnormal{\itshape symmetric (Right or Left-Right rotation)}
\end{codebox}

% ==================================================================
\section{Exam-Style Practice}

\begin{exambox}[Practice: insert triggering a double rotation]
Start from the AVL tree of $\{10, 20, 30, 40, 50\}$ built in order, then \textbf{Insert 25}. Show the rebalancing.

\begin{answerband}
Inserting $10,20,30$ forces one left rotation, giving root $20$. After $40, 50$ (another left rotation at $30$) the tree is:
$$20 \to \{\, 10,\; 40 \to \{30, 50\}\,\}$$

\textbf{Insert 25:} search path $20 \to 40 \to 30$, land as \textbf{left child of 30}.

Walk up: $\text{BF}(30): 0 \to -1$, \; $\text{BF}(40): 0 \to -1$, \; $\text{BF}(20): +1 \to \textcolor{rustW}{+2}$.

$v=20$ has $\text{BF}=+2$ and $\text{BF}(v.\text{right})=\text{BF}(40)=-1$ $\Rightarrow$ \textbf{Right-Left rotation}: right-rotate at $40$, then left-rotate at $20$. Middle key $30$ rises:
$$30 \to \{\, 20 \to \{10, 25\},\; 40 \to \{\emptyset, 50\}\,\}$$

All $|\text{BF}| \le 1$. In-order: $10,20,25,30,40,50$ \textcolor{greenO}{$\checkmark$}
\end{answerband}
\end{exambox}

\begin{warnbox}[On past exams]
AVL questions in this course ask you to \textbf{draw the tree after an insert/delete and name each rotation} (see the Fall 2014 boxes in Lecture 5). The skill tested is exactly the case decision above: $\text{BF}(v)$ plus the heavy child's BF picks single vs double.
\end{warnbox}

% ==================================================================
% ==================================================================
\section{Midterm-Style Process Problems}

\begin{examplebox}[Predicted: compute $m(h)$ and bound the height]
Let $m(h)$ be the minimum number of nodes in an AVL tree of height $h$. Using $m(0)=1$, $m(1)=2$, $m(h)=1+m(h-1)+m(h-2)$, fill the table through $h=6$, and explain why this forces height $=O(\log n)$.

\begin{answerband}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.25}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|c c c c c c c}
$h$ & 0 & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5 & 6\\
\hline
$m(h)$ & 1 & 2 & 4 & 7 & 12 & 20 & 33\\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Each value is $1$ plus the previous two, so $m(h)$ grows like the \textbf{Fibonacci} numbers, that is \emph{exponentially} in $h$. Since an AVL tree of height $h$ has $n\geq m(h)$ nodes and $m(h)$ grows exponentially, $h$ can only grow like $\log n$. Precisely, $\text{height}\leq 1.44\log_2(n+2)$, so every AVL operation is \textcolor{violetT}{$O(\log n)$}.
\end{answerband}
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}[Predicted MC: identify the rotation]
The AVL tree on the left became the one on the right after an insertion. Which rotation was applied at $A$?

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[every node/.style={circle, draw=heapblue, line width=0.7pt, minimum size=7mm, inner sep=1pt, font=\small, fill=white}]
\node[font=\bfseries\scriptsize, draw=none, fill=none] at (0.0,0.8) {Before:};
\node[draw=rustW,line width=1pt,label={[rustW,font=\tiny\bfseries]right:{$-2$}}] (A) at (0.8,0) {A};
\node[draw=rustW,line width=1pt,label={[rustW,font=\tiny\bfseries]left:{$+1$}}] (B) at (-0.2,-1.1) {B};
\node (T4) at (1.8,-1.1) {$T_4$};
\node (T1) at (-1.2,-2.2) {$T_1$};
\node[draw=rustW,line width=1pt] (C) at (0.8,-2.2) {C};
\node (T2) at (0.2,-3.3) {$T_2$}; \node (T3) at (1.4,-3.3) {$T_3$};
\draw (A)--(B); \draw (A)--(T4); \draw (B)--(T1); \draw (B)--(C); \draw (C)--(T2); \draw (C)--(T3);
\draw[-{Stealth}, line width=1.1pt, rustW] (2.6,-1.6) -- (4.0,-1.6);
\node[font=\bfseries\scriptsize, draw=none, fill=none] at (4.6,0.8) {After:};
\node[label={[greenO,font=\tiny\bfseries]above right:{$0$}}] (C2) at (5.8,0) {C};
\node (B2) at (4.8,-1.1) {B}; \node (A2) at (6.8,-1.1) {A};
\node (T1b) at (4.1,-2.2) {$T_1$}; \node (T2b) at (5.3,-2.2) {$T_2$};
\node (T3b) at (6.3,-2.2) {$T_3$}; \node (T4b) at (7.3,-2.2) {$T_4$};
\draw (C2)--(B2); \draw (C2)--(A2); \draw (B2)--(T1b); \draw (B2)--(T2b); \draw (A2)--(T3b); \draw (A2)--(T4b);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}
(a) single Right at $A$ (LL) \quad (b) single Left at $A$ (RR) \quad (c) Left on $B$ then Right at $A$ (LR) \quad (d) Right on $B$ then Left at $A$ (RL)

\begin{answerband}
\textbf{(c) LR double.} $A$ is left-heavy ($-2$) and its left child $B$ is right-heavy ($+1$), a zig-zag, so left-rotate $B$ then right-rotate $A$. The middle node $C$ rises to the top with $B$ and $A$ as its children.
\end{answerband}
\end{examplebox}

\section*{\textcolor{violetT}{Quick Reference: Lecture 6}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quick Reference: Lecture 6}
\begin{keybox}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.4}
\begin{tabular}{l l}
First unbalanced node $A$ & lowest node with $|\text{BF}|=2$ after insert; assume $+2$\\
$B = A.\text{right}$ & grew to height $h+1$; $\text{BF}(B)\neq 0$ (proved)\\
Case 1a ($+2,+1$, RR) & single Left rotation; $A,B \to 0$\\
Case 1b ($+2,-1$, RL) & Right-Left double; $C$ rises, $\text{BF}(C)=0$\\
$h=-1$ special case & inserted leaf becomes the new subtree root\\
Rotation cost & 3 pointer pairs $\Rightarrow \Theta(1)$\\
Height after fix & equals pre-insert height $\Rightarrow$ at most 1 rotation\\
Node fields & PARENT, key, BF, LEFT CHILD, RIGHT CHILD\\
\textbf{Insert} total & BST insert + walk-up + $\le 1$ rotation $\to \Theta(\log n)$\\
\end{tabular}
\end{keybox}

\part*{\textcolor{utnavy}{Lecture 7: Augmenting Data Structures}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{Lecture 7: Augmenting Data Structures}
\setcounter{section}{0}

% ==================================================================
\section{Augmenting a Data Structure}

\begin{defbox}[Augmenting a data structure]
Take an \textbf{existing} data structure and add \textbf{extra stored information} so it supports \textbf{new operations}, without breaking the old ones. Three steps:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item Decide \textbf{what extra info} to store at each node.
  \item Check the info can be \textbf{cheaply maintained} under every existing operation.
  \item Use the info to \textbf{implement the new operations} efficiently.
\end{enumerate}
Not automatic: choosing the right field takes \textbf{creativity}.
\end{defbox}

% ==================================================================
\section{Dynamic Order Statistics}

\begin{defbox}[Dynamic Order Statistics ADT]
A dynamic set $S$ of elements with \textbf{distinct keys}, supporting:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item \textbf{Search$(x)$, Insert$(x)$, Delete$(x)$} \hfill (the Dictionary operations)
  \item \textbf{Select$(k)$}: return the \textcolor{rustW}{$k$-th smallest} element (the element of \textbf{rank} $k$)
  \item \textbf{Rank$(x)$}: given a pointer to $x$, return its \textcolor{rustW}{rank}
\end{itemize}
\textbf{Rank of $x$} = position of $x$ in the sorted order of $S$ (starting at $1$).
\end{defbox}

\subsection{Example}

\begin{examplebox}[Select and Rank on $S=\{5,15,27,30,56\}$]
\begin{center}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.3}
\begin{tabular}{l l l l}
\textbf{Select}$(4) = 30$ & \textbf{Select}$(1) = 5$ & \textbf{Rank}$(15) = 2$ & \textbf{Rank}$(30) = 4$\\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
$\text{Rank}(30)=4$ because \textbf{exactly 3} elements are $< 30$ (namely $5, 15, 27$), so $30$ sits in position $4$.
\end{examplebox}

\subsection{Naive Attempt: Store Each Node's Rank}

Keep $S$ in an \textbf{AVL tree} (Search/Insert/Delete already $O(\log n)$). To get Select/Rank, augment it. First idea: at each node store its \textbf{rank}.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  every node/.style={circle, draw=heapblue, line width=0.7pt, minimum size=7mm, inner sep=1pt, font=\small, fill=white},
  lbl/.style={draw=none, fill=none, font=\scriptsize\bfseries, text=rustW}
]
\node (n15) {15}; \node[lbl] at (0.55,0.45) {2};
\node (n5) at (-1.6,-1.2) {5}; \node[lbl] at (-1.05,-0.9) {1};
\node (n30) at (1.6,-1.2) {30}; \node[lbl] at (2.15,-0.9) {4};
\node (n27) at (0.6,-2.4) {27}; \node[lbl] at (1.15,-2.1) {3};
\node (n56) at (2.6,-2.4) {56}; \node[lbl] at (3.15,-2.1) {5};
\draw (n15)--(n5); \draw (n15)--(n30); \draw (n30)--(n27); \draw (n30)--(n56);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

\begin{keybox}[Good, but\ldots]
\textcolor{greenO}{Good:} Rank$(x)$ reads the stored field ($O(1)$ after finding $x$), and Select$(k)$ searches for the node whose rank is $k$ in $O(\log n)$.
\end{keybox}

\begin{warnbox}[Why this fails: $O(n)$ updates]
\textbf{Insert$(2)$} (a new minimum) makes $2$ rank $1$ and \textbf{shifts every other rank up by 1}. One insert can rewrite \textbf{all $n$} rank fields $\Rightarrow$ Insert and Delete become \textcolor{rustW}{$O(n)$}. The rank field is \textbf{too global} to maintain cheaply.
\end{warnbox}

% ==================================================================
\section{Size-Augmented AVL Tree}

\subsection{The \texttt{size} Field}

\begin{defbox}[Store subtree size]
At each node $x$ store $\texttt{size}(x)$ = number of nodes in the subtree rooted at $x$. Invariant, for every node:
\begin{center}\large
$\texttt{size}(x) = \texttt{size}(\text{left}(x)) + \texttt{size}(\text{right}(x)) + 1$
\end{center}
(An empty child contributes $\texttt{size}=0$.) This is a \textbf{local} field: it depends only on $x$'s two children, so a single insert changes it on just \textbf{one root-to-leaf path}.
\end{defbox}

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  every node/.style={circle, draw=heapblue, line width=0.7pt, minimum size=7mm, inner sep=1pt, font=\small, fill=white},
  lbl/.style={draw=none, fill=none, font=\scriptsize\bfseries, text=greenO}
]
\node (n15) {15}; \node[lbl] at (0.6,0.45) {5};
\node (n5) at (-1.6,-1.2) {5}; \node[lbl] at (-1.05,-0.9) {1};
\node (n30) at (1.6,-1.2) {30}; \node[lbl] at (2.2,-0.9) {3};
\node (n27) at (0.6,-2.4) {27}; \node[lbl] at (1.15,-2.1) {1};
\node (n56) at (2.6,-2.4) {56}; \node[lbl] at (3.15,-2.1) {1};
\draw (n15)--(n5); \draw (n15)--(n30); \draw (n30)--(n27); \draw (n30)--(n56);
\node[draw=none,fill=none,font=\scriptsize,text=greenO,anchor=west] at (3.4,-1.2) {(green = \texttt{size})};
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

\subsection{Select: the $k$-th Smallest}

\begin{defbox}[Relative rank RR($x$)]
$\text{RR}(x)$ = the rank of $x$ counting \textbf{only the nodes in $x$'s own subtree}. In other words, if you sorted just $x$'s subtree, $\text{RR}(x)$ is the position $x$ would land in.
$$\text{RR}(x) = \texttt{size}(\text{left}(x)) + 1$$
\end{defbox}

\begin{keybox}[Why that formula works]
In a BST, the \textbf{left subtree of $x$ holds exactly the elements smaller than $x$} (within $x$'s subtree). So:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item $\texttt{size}(\text{left}(x))$ = how many elements are smaller than $x$ here.
  \item $+\,1$ = counts $x$ itself.
\end{itemize}
Add them and you get $x$'s position. No traversal needed: the \texttt{size} field already has the count.
\end{keybox}

\begin{examplebox}[Reading RR straight off the tree]
\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  every node/.style={circle, draw=heapblue, line width=0.7pt, minimum size=7mm, inner sep=1pt, font=\small, fill=white},
  lbl/.style={draw=none, fill=none, font=\scriptsize\bfseries, text=greenO}
]
\node (n30) {30}; \node[lbl] at (0.6,0.45) {5};
\node (n15) at (-1.6,-1.2) {15}; \node[lbl] at (-1.05,-0.9) {3};
\node (n56) at (1.6,-1.2) {56}; \node[lbl] at (2.15,-0.9) {1};
\node (n5) at (-2.6,-2.4) {5}; \node[lbl] at (-2.05,-2.1) {1};
\node (n27) at (-0.6,-2.4) {27}; \node[lbl] at (-0.05,-2.1) {1};
\draw (n30)--(n15); \draw (n30)--(n56); \draw (n15)--(n5); \draw (n15)--(n27);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}
Green numbers are \texttt{size}. Read RR off each node:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item $\text{RR}(30) = \texttt{size}(\text{left}{=}15) + 1 = 3 + 1 = 4$. So $30$ is the \textbf{4th smallest} in this whole subtree. Check: $5,15,27,\mathbf{30},56$ \checkmark
  \item $\text{RR}(15) = \texttt{size}(\text{left}{=}5) + 1 = 1 + 1 = 2$. So $15$ is the \textbf{2nd smallest} in the subtree under $15$ ($\{5,15,27\}$). Check: $5,\mathbf{15},27$ \checkmark
  \item $\text{RR}(5) = 0 + 1 = 1$ (no left child). $5$ is smallest in its subtree. \checkmark
\end{itemize}
Notice RR is \textbf{local}: $\text{RR}(15)=2$ is its rank inside $\{5,15,27\}$, not its rank $2$ in the full set. Select stitches these local ranks together as it walks down.
\end{examplebox}

\begin{codebox}[Select(x, k): the $k$-th smallest in the subtree at $x$]
\ttfamily RR $\leftarrow$ size(left(x)) + 1\\
\ttfamily if k = RR:\, return x \hfill\textnormal{\itshape // x sits exactly at position k}\\
\ttfamily if k < RR:\, return Select(left(x), k) \hfill\textnormal{\itshape // target is smaller, look left}\\
\ttfamily if k > RR:\, return Select(right(x), k $-$ RR) \hfill\textnormal{\itshape // skip x and all of left}
\end{codebox}

Call \texttt{Select(T, k) = Select(root, k)}.

\begin{keybox}[The one tricky line: why subtract when going right]
Going right means the answer is bigger than $x$, so it is \textbf{not} $x$ and not anyone in $x$'s left subtree. That is $\text{RR}$ elements we are leaving behind. Inside the right subtree those elements no longer exist, so the thing that was rank $k$ overall becomes rank $k - \text{RR}$ \emph{there}. That is why we recurse with $k - \text{RR}$, not $k$.
\end{keybox}

\begin{examplebox}[Trace: Select(3) on $S=\{5,15,27,30,56\}$]
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item At root $15$: $\text{RR}=\texttt{size}(\text{left}=5)+1 = 1+1 = 2$. \; $k=3 > 2 \Rightarrow$ go right, $k \leftarrow 3-2 = 1$.
  \item At $30$: $\text{RR}=\texttt{size}(\text{left}=27)+1 = 1+1 = 2$. \; $k=1 < 2 \Rightarrow$ go left.
  \item At $27$: $\text{RR}=0+1=1$. \; $k=1 = \text{RR} \Rightarrow$ \textcolor{greenO}{return $27$.}
\end{enumerate}
$\text{Select}(3)=27$ \checkmark (sorted: $5,15,\mathbf{27},30,56$).
\end{examplebox}

\begin{keybox}[Cost of Select]
Each call drops one level (or returns), $O(1)$ per level, height $O(\log n)$ $\Rightarrow$ \textcolor{violetT}{$O(\log n)$}.
\end{keybox}

\subsection{Rank of a Node}

Given a pointer to $x$, walk \textbf{from $x$ up to the root}, accumulating the elements that come before $x$.

\begin{codebox}[Rank(T, x): rank of x in the whole tree]
\ttfamily RR $\leftarrow$ size(left(x)) + 1 \hfill\textnormal{\itshape // rank of x within its own subtree}\\
\ttfamily y $\leftarrow$ x\\
\ttfamily while y $\ne$ root:\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{2em}if y is the RIGHT child of its parent p:\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{4em}RR $\leftarrow$ RR + size(left(p)) + 1 \hfill\textnormal{\itshape // p and its left subtree precede x}\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{2em}y $\leftarrow$ p\\
\ttfamily return RR
\end{codebox}

\begin{keybox}[Why]
Climbing to parent $p$: if we came up from $p$'s \textbf{right} subtree, then $p$ itself and all of $p$'s \textbf{left} subtree are smaller than $x$, so add $\texttt{size}(\text{left}(p))+1$. If we came up from the \textbf{left}, those nodes are all larger, so $\text{RR}$ is \textbf{unchanged}.
\end{keybox}

\begin{examplebox}[Accumulating up the path]
Suppose $\text{RR}(x)=7$ inside $x$'s subtree. Climbing through ancestors where $x$ sits in the right subtree, with left-subtree sizes $3$, then $6$:
$$7 \;\to\; 7 + 3 + 1 = 11 \;\to\; 11 + 6 + 1 = 18.$$
Each right-turn adds ``that ancestor $+$ its left subtree.'' Left-turns add nothing.
\end{examplebox}

\begin{keybox}[Cost of Rank]
One step per level, $O(1)$ each, height $O(\log n)$ $\Rightarrow$ \textcolor{violetT}{$O(\log n)$}.
\end{keybox}

\subsection{Maintaining \texttt{size} on Insert and Delete}

\begin{codebox}[Insert(T, x): keep \texttt{size} correct]
\ttfamily \textbf{Phase 1 (BST insert):} insert x as a leaf; size(x) = 1;\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{2em}for each node y on the path from the root down to x:\, size(y) += 1\\[3pt]
\ttfamily \textbf{Phase 2 (rebalance):} walk up adjusting BF; rotate if some BF hits $\pm 2$.\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{2em}After a rotation, recompute size() of the rotated nodes\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{2em}bottom-up: size(z) = size(left(z)) + size(right(z)) + 1
\end{codebox}

\begin{keybox}[Why it stays $O(\log n)$]
Phase 1 touches one root-to-leaf path ($O(\log n)$ increments). A rotation only changes the children of \textbf{2 nodes}, so recomputing their \texttt{size} from the invariant is \textcolor{greenO}{$O(1)$} extra. AVL insert already does at most one rotation $\Rightarrow$ total \textcolor{violetT}{$O(\log n)$}. \textbf{Delete} is symmetric (decrement down the path; fix \texttt{size} after each rotation).
\end{keybox}

\begin{examplebox}[Fixing \texttt{size} after a left rotation]
A left rotation at $A$ (right child $B$ rises). Only $A$ and $B$ changed children, so recompute \textbf{bottom-up}:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item $A$ (now lower): $\texttt{size}(A) = \texttt{size}(T_1) + \texttt{size}(T_2) + 1$.
  \item $B$ (now on top): $\texttt{size}(B) = \texttt{size}(A) + \texttt{size}(T_3) + 1$.
\end{itemize}
Everything below $T_1, T_2, T_3$ is untouched, so two updates suffice.
\end{examplebox}

% ==================================================================
\section{Summary and Practice}

\begin{keybox}[Size-augmented AVL: all operations $O(\log n)$]
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.35}
\begin{tabular}{l l l}
\textbf{Search / Insert / Delete} & \textcolor{violetT}{$O(\log n)$} & AVL + fix \texttt{size} on the path\\
\textbf{Select$(k)$} & \textcolor{violetT}{$O(\log n)$} & descend using $\text{RR}=\texttt{size}(\text{left})+1$\\
\textbf{Rank$(x)$} & \textcolor{violetT}{$O(\log n)$} & climb, add $\texttt{size}(\text{left}(p))+1$ on right-turns\\
\end{tabular}
\end{keybox}

\begin{exambox}[Practice: Select and Rank]
Augmented AVL for $S=\{5,15,27,30,56\}$ (root $15$; $30$ has children $27, 56$). Do \textbf{Select$(5)$} and \textbf{Rank$(27)$}.

\begin{answerband}
\textbf{Select$(5)$:} root $15$, $\text{RR}=2$, $5>2 \Rightarrow$ right, $k=3$. At $30$, $\text{RR}=2$, $3>2 \Rightarrow$ right, $k=1$. At $56$, $\text{RR}=1=k \Rightarrow$ \textcolor{greenO}{return $56$}.

\textbf{Rank$(27)$:} start $\text{RR}=\texttt{size}(\text{left}(27))+1 = 0+1 = 1$. Climb to $30$: $27$ is $30$'s \textbf{left} child $\Rightarrow$ unchanged. Climb to $15$: $30$ is $15$'s \textbf{right} child $\Rightarrow$ $\text{RR} \leftarrow 1 + \texttt{size}(\text{left}(15){=}5) + 1 = 1+1+1 = 3$. \textcolor{greenO}{Rank$(27)=3$} \checkmark
\end{answerband}
\end{exambox}

% ==================================================================
% ==================================================================
\section{Midterm-Style Process Problems}

\begin{examplebox}[Predicted: OS-Select on a size-augmented tree]
Each node stores $\text{size}=$ number of nodes in its subtree (shown as a subscript). Run \textsc{OS-Select}$(5)$, the $5$th smallest key.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
   level distance=1.05cm,
   every node/.style={circle, draw=heapblue, line width=0.7pt, minimum size=8mm, inner sep=0pt, font=\scriptsize, fill=white},
   level 1/.style={sibling distance=3.0cm}, level 2/.style={sibling distance=1.4cm}
]
\node {$26_{\,7}$}
  child {node {$17_{\,3}$} child {node {$14_{\,1}$}} child {node {$21_{\,1}$}} }
  child {node {$41_{\,3}$} child {node {$30_{\,1}$}} child {node {$47_{\,1}$}} };
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

\begin{answerband}
At each node compute $r=\text{size}(\text{left})+1$, the rank of this node inside its own subtree.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.6em,itemsep=2pt]
  \item At $26$: $r=\text{size}(17)+1=3+1=4$. Want $i=5>4$, so go \textbf{right} and set $i=5-4=1$.
  \item At $41$: $r=\text{size}(30)+1=1+1=2$. Want $i=1<2$, so go \textbf{left}.
  \item At $30$: $r=\text{size}(\text{nil})+1=1$. Want $i=1=r$: \textcolor{greenO}{\textbf{return }$30$}.
\end{enumerate}
In-order the keys are $14,17,21,26,30,41,47$, and indeed the $5$th is $\mathbf{30}$. One root-to-node path, so $O(\log n)$.
\end{answerband}
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}[Predicted: design an augmentation]
You have a set $S$ of integers in an AVL tree. Design an augmentation so that $\textsc{SumLeq}(t)=$ ``sum of all keys $\leq t$'' runs in $O(\log n)$, and \textsc{Insert}/\textsc{Delete} stay $O(\log n)$.

\begin{answerband}
\textbf{Augment} each node $u$ with $\text{size}(u)$ and $\text{sum}(u)=$ sum of all keys in $u$'s subtree. Both satisfy a local rule from the children:
\[
  \text{sum}(u)=\text{sum}(\text{left})+\text{key}(u)+\text{sum}(\text{right}),
\]
so they are maintainable in $O(1)$ extra work per node during the usual \textsc{Insert}/\textsc{Delete} walk and rotations (a rotation touches a constant number of nodes).

\textbf{Query.} Walk down from the root: at node $u$, if $t<\text{key}(u)$ recurse left; else add $\text{sum}(\text{left})+\text{key}(u)$ to the running total and recurse right. This visits one path, so \textsc{SumLeq} is \textcolor{violetT}{$O(\log n)$}. (Dividing \textsc{SumLeq}$(t)$ by a size count gives an $O(\log n)$ \textsc{Average} query, the same trick as past exams.)
\end{answerband}
\end{examplebox}

\section*{\textcolor{violetT}{Quick Reference: Lecture 7}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quick Reference: Lecture 7}
\begin{keybox}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.4}
\begin{tabular}{l l}
Augmenting (3 steps) & pick field; keep it cheap to maintain; use it for new ops\\
Order statistics & Dictionary + Select$(k)$ ($k$-th smallest) + Rank$(x)$\\
Naive (store rank) & Select/Rank easy but Insert/Delete $O(n)$ (too global)\\
Fix: store \texttt{size}$(x)$ & $\texttt{size}(x)=\texttt{size}(\text{left})+\texttt{size}(\text{right})+1$ (local)\\
$\text{RR}(x)$ & $\texttt{size}(\text{left}(x))+1$ = rank of $x$ within its subtree\\
Select$(x,k)$ & $=$RR: return; $<$: go left; $>$: go right with $k-\text{RR}$\\
Rank$(T,x)$ & climb to root; right-child step adds $\texttt{size}(\text{left}(p))+1$\\
Maintain \texttt{size} & $+1$ down insert path; recompute 2 nodes per rotation ($O(1)$)\\
All operations & \textcolor{violetT}{$O(\log n)$}\\
\end{tabular}
\end{keybox}
