\part*{\textcolor{utnavy}{Lecture 8: Hash Tables}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{Lecture 8: Hash Tables}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Recall: the Dictionary ADT}

\begin{defbox}[Dictionary ADT]
Object: a dynamic set $S$. Each element has a \textcolor{rustW}{key} $k \in U$, where $U$ is the \textbf{universe} of possible keys.

Operations: \textcolor{heapblue}{\textbf{Search}}, \textcolor{heapblue}{\textbf{Insert}}, \textcolor{heapblue}{\textbf{Delete}}.
\end{defbox}

We already saw BSTs and AVL trees give $O(\log n)$ per operation. Hashing aims for \textcolor{greenO}{$\Theta(1)$ expected} per operation.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{If the Universe $U$ is Small: Direct-Access Table}

\begin{defbox}[Direct-access table]
If $U = \{0, 1, \ldots, u-1\}$ is small, use an array $T[0 \ldots u-1]$. Store the element with key $k$ directly in slot $T[k]$. Empty slots hold \texttt{NIL}.
\end{defbox}

\begin{keybox}[Direct-access operations: all \textcolor{greenO}{$\Theta(1)$} worst case]
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.4}
\begin{tabular}{l l}
\textcolor{heapblue}{\textbf{Search}$(T, k)$} & return $T[k]$ \\
\textcolor{heapblue}{\textbf{Insert}$(T, x)$} & $T[k] \leftarrow x$ \quad (where $k$ is $x$'s key) \\
\textcolor{heapblue}{\textbf{Delete}$(T, x)$} & $T[k] \leftarrow \texttt{NIL}$ \\
\end{tabular}
\end{keybox}

\begin{examplebox}[Why it works: counting character frequencies]
Read a text file, count how often each character appears. Each character has an ASCII code in $\{0, \ldots, 127\}$. Use $T[0 \ldots 127]$, all entries start at $0$. For each character with code $k$, increment $T[k]$. Then $T[k]$ is the frequency of the character with code $k$.

Total time $\textcolor{rustW}{\Theta(n)}$ where $n$ is the length of the text. Each character costs $\Theta(1)$.
\end{examplebox}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{When the Universe is Huge}

\begin{warnbox}[Direct-access tables break down]
Suppose keys are 64-bit integers: $|U| = 2^{64} \approx 1.84 \times 10^{19}$. A table of size $2^{64}$ is \textbf{infeasible}. Usually we only store $n \ll |U|$ keys, so almost every slot would be wasted.
\end{warnbox}

\textbf{Fix:} use a table of size $m$ that is close to $n$ (not $|U|$), and a function that maps any key into $\{0, \ldots, m-1\}$. This is a \textcolor{rustW}{hash table}.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Hash Tables}

\begin{defbox}[\textcolor{defblue}{Hash table (MUST KNOW)}]
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item $m$ = size of the table $T[0 \ldots m-1]$.
  \item \textbf{Hash function} $h : U \to \{0, 1, \ldots, m-1\}$.
  \item For a key $k$, if $h(k) = i$ we say ``\textit{$k$ hashes to slot $i$}''.
  \item Store the element with key $k$ in slot $T[h(k)]$.
\end{itemize}
\end{defbox}

\begin{warnbox}[What could go wrong: collisions]
A \textcolor{rustW}{collision} is when two different keys hash to the same slot: $h(k_1) = h(k_3)$. Since $|U| > m$, by pigeonhole \textbf{some pair of keys must collide}. We cannot avoid collisions, only resolve them.
\end{warnbox}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Resolving Collisions: Chaining}

\begin{defbox}[Chaining]
Keep a \textbf{linked list} at each table slot. To insert the element with key $k$, add it at the \textbf{head} of the list in slot $T[h(k)]$. All keys that hash to the same slot sit in the same list (chain).
\end{defbox}

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  box/.style={draw=heapblue, line width=0.7pt, minimum width=1.4cm, minimum height=0.55cm, font=\small, fill=white},
  tslot/.style={draw=heapblue, line width=0.7pt, minimum width=0.9cm, minimum height=0.55cm, fill=skyfill}
]
% table column
\node[tslot] (ti) at (0,0) {};
\node[tslot] (td) at (0,-0.55) {$\vdots$};
\node[tslot] (tj) at (0,-1.1) {};
\node[font=\scriptsize\bfseries, rustW] at (-0.75,0) {$i$};
\node[font=\scriptsize\bfseries, rustW] at (-0.75,-1.1) {$j$};
\node[font=\bfseries] at (0,0.55) {$T$};
% chain at i: k4 -> k3 -> k1
\node[box] (k4) at (2.0,0) {$k_4$};
\node[box] (k3) at (3.7,0) {$k_3$};
\node[box] (k1) at (5.4,0) {$k_1$};
\draw[{Stealth}-{Stealth}, heapblue] (ti.east) -- (k4.west);
\draw[{Stealth}-{Stealth}, heapblue] (k4.east) -- (k3.west);
\draw[{Stealth}-{Stealth}, heapblue] (k3.east) -- (k1.west);
% chain at j: k2
\node[box] (k2) at (2.0,-1.1) {$k_2$};
\draw[{Stealth}-{Stealth}, heapblue] (tj.east) -- (k2.west);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

Here $h(k_1) = h(k_3) = h(k_4) = i$ and $h(k_2) = j$. New keys go in at the head, so $k_4$ (inserted last) is first in slot $i$'s chain.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Dictionary Operations with Chaining}

\begin{keybox}[Costs (assume doubly linked lists, and $h(k)$ computable in \textcolor{greenO}{$\Theta(1)$})]
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.5}
\begin{tabular}{l l l}
\textcolor{heapblue}{\textbf{Insert}$(x)$} & insert at head of $T[h(\text{key}(x))]$ & \textcolor{greenO}{$\Theta(1)$}\\
\textcolor{heapblue}{\textbf{Delete}$(x)$} & remove $x$, given a pointer to $x$ & \textcolor{greenO}{$\Theta(1)$}\\
\textcolor{heapblue}{\textbf{Search}$(k)$} & scan the chain at $T[h(k)]$ & \textcolor{rustW}{depends on chain length}\\
\end{tabular}
\end{keybox}

\begin{warnbox}[Worst case for Search]
All $n$ keys could hash to the \textbf{same} slot. Then that one chain has length $n$, and Search takes \textcolor{rustW}{$\Theta(n)$} in the worst case. So we analyse the \textbf{average} chain length instead.
\end{warnbox}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Analysis: SUHA and the Load Factor}

\begin{defbox}[Simple Uniform Hashing Assumption (SUHA)]
Each key $k$ we insert is \textbf{equally likely} to hash into any of the $m$ slots, \textbf{independently} of the other keys:
\[
  \Pr[h(k) = i] = \tfrac{1}{m}, \qquad 0 \le i \le m-1.
\]
\end{defbox}

\begin{keybox}[Expected chain length $=$ load factor $\alpha$]
Insert $n$ keys into an empty table $T[0 \ldots m-1]$. Let $n_i$ be the length of the chain at $T[i]$ (a random variable). The chains partition all keys, so
\[
  n_0 + n_1 + \cdots + n_{m-1} = n
  \;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;
  E[n_0] + \cdots + E[n_{m-1}] = n.
\]
By symmetry under SUHA all $E[n_i]$ are equal, so $m \cdot E[n_i] = n$, giving
\[
  \boxed{\,E[n_i] = \frac{n}{m} = \alpha\,} \qquad \text{the \textcolor{violetT}{load factor} of } T.
\]
\end{keybox}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Expected Cost of Search (under SUHA)}

\begin{keybox}[Expected Search time is \textcolor{greenO}{$\Theta(1 + \alpha)$}]
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.4}
\begin{tabular}{l l}
\textcolor{rustW}{\textbf{Unsuccessful}} & inspect a whole chain: $\alpha$ comparisons on average\\
\textcolor{greenO}{\textbf{Successful}} & inspect about half a chain, then match: $\tfrac{\alpha}{2} + 1$ on average\\
\end{tabular}
\medskip

The ``$+1$'' is the cost of computing $h(k)$ and indexing, which happens even when $\alpha = 0$. So expected Search time is \textcolor{greenO}{$\Theta(1 + \alpha)$}.
\end{keybox}

\begin{keybox}[Keeping Search fast: fix $\alpha$]
If $n$ keeps growing while $m$ is fixed, $\alpha = n/m$ grows and chains get longer. Keep $\alpha$ \textbf{bounded by a constant} by ensuring $m = \Theta(n)$. Then $\alpha = \Theta(1)$ and
\[
  \text{Expected Search time} = \textcolor{greenO}{\Theta(1)}.
\]
\end{keybox}

\begin{keybox}[The table may have to grow (resizing)]
To keep $\alpha \le c$ for a chosen constant $c$:
\[
  \frac{n}{m} \le c \;\Longleftrightarrow\; m \ge \frac{n}{c}.
\]
When $n$ grows past $c\,m$: (1) allocate a new, larger table so $\alpha \le c$ holds again, then (2) \textbf{rehash} every current element into it.
\end{keybox}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Choosing a Hash Function}

\begin{defbox}[Division method]
\begin{center}\large $h(k) = k \bmod m$\end{center}
Common heuristic: choose $m$ to be \textbf{prime}.
\end{defbox}

\begin{examplebox}[Picking $m$]
We want to store $n = 2000$ keys with target $\alpha \le 3$.
\[
  \alpha = \frac{2000}{m} \le 3 \;\Longrightarrow\; m \ge \frac{2000}{3} \approx 666.7.
\]
Smallest qualifying prime: $\textcolor{rustW}{673}$. Use $m = 673$ and $h(k) = k \bmod 673$.
\end{examplebox}

\begin{warnbox}[Why not just any $m$?]
If $m = 2^{\ell}$, then $k \bmod m$ uses \textbf{only the last $\ell$ bits} of $k$. Extreme case $m = 2$: even keys go to slot $0$, odd keys to slot $1$. If most keys are even, almost all land in slot $0$: a very uneven distribution.

A good hash function uses \textbf{all the bits} of the key and spreads keys \textbf{evenly} across slots, even when the inserted keys follow some regular pattern.
\end{warnbox}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Past Exam Questions}

\begin{exambox}[Seen on a past exam \textnormal{(Final, Dec 2013, Q1: Hash Tables)}]
\textbf{[18 marks]} Hash tables with chaining; $m$ = table size, $n$ = number of items.

\textbf{(a)} What issue with the \textit{direct-access table} does the \textit{hash table} solve?\\
\textbf{(b)} To store $n$ items, what is a good choice for $m$?\\
\textbf{(c)} What is the purpose of chaining?\\
\textbf{(d)} What property should a good hash function have?\\
\textbf{(e)} Assume SUHA. Fill the running-time table (tight bounds, only $n$ and $m$):

\begin{answerband}
\textbf{(a)} A direct-access table needs size $|U|$, which is infeasible when $U$ is huge (e.g. 64-bit keys). A hash table uses a small table of size $m = \Theta(n)$ and maps keys into it with $h$.

\textbf{(b)} $m = \Theta(n)$, chosen prime (keeps $\alpha = n/m$ constant).

\textbf{(c)} Chaining \textbf{resolves collisions}: all keys hashing to the same slot are kept in one linked list at that slot.

\textbf{(d)} It should spread keys \textbf{evenly} across all $m$ slots and \textbf{use all the bits} of the key, so chains stay short even for patterned inputs.

\smallskip
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.5}
\begin{tabular}{l c c c}
\hline
 & \textbf{Search}$(k)$ & \textbf{Insert}$(k)$ & \textbf{Delete}$(x)$\\
\hline
\textbf{Worst case} & \textcolor{rustW}{$\Theta(n)$} & \textcolor{greenO}{$\Theta(1)$} & \textcolor{greenO}{$\Theta(1)$}\\
\textbf{Expected} & \textcolor{greenO}{$\Theta(1 + \alpha) = \Theta(1)$} & \textcolor{greenO}{$\Theta(1)$} & \textcolor{greenO}{$\Theta(1)$}\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\smallskip

(Delete is $\Theta(1)$ given a pointer to $x$ and doubly linked chains. Expected Search uses $m = \Theta(n)$ so $\alpha = \Theta(1)$.)
\end{answerband}
\end{exambox}

\begin{exambox}[Seen on a past exam \textnormal{(Final, Dec 2013, Q1e)}]
\textbf{[4 marks]} A hash table $T$ of size $1000$ uses chaining, starts empty, SUHA holds. What is the \textbf{maximum} number of keys we can insert so that, afterwards, the \textbf{expected} number of key comparisons to search for a key \textbf{not} in $T$ is at most $15$?

\begin{answerband}
An unsuccessful search inspects a whole chain, so its expected number of comparisons is $\alpha = \dfrac{n}{m} = \dfrac{n}{1000}$. Require
\[
  \frac{n}{1000} \le 15 \;\Longrightarrow\; n \le 15000.
\]
\textcolor{violetT}{\textbf{Maximum} $= 15000$ keys.}
\end{answerband}
\end{exambox}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
% ==================================================================
\section{Midterm-Style Process Problems}

\begin{examplebox}[Predicted: build a chained table, then analyse]
A chained hash table has $m=5$ slots and $h(k)=k\bmod 5$. Insert, \textbf{at the head} of each chain, the keys $10,7,23,5,18,12,36$. Draw the table, then give the load factor and expected search cost under SUHA.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  slot/.style={draw,line width=0.6pt,minimum width=0.9cm,minimum height=0.72cm,font=\small},
  cell/.style={draw=heapblue,line width=0.7pt,fill=white,minimum width=0.8cm,minimum height=0.62cm,font=\small},
  link/.style={-{Stealth},heapblue,line width=0.7pt}
]
\foreach \s in {0,1,2,3,4}{ \node[slot] (s\s) at (0,-\s*0.95) {\s}; }
\node[cell] (a1) at (1.5,0) {5}; \node[cell] (a2) at (2.7,0) {10};
\draw[link] (s0.east) -- (a1.west); \draw[link] (a1.east) -- (a2.west);
\node[cell] (b1) at (1.5,-0.95) {36}; \draw[link] (s1.east) -- (b1.west);
\node[cell] (c1) at (1.5,-1.9) {12}; \node[cell] (c2) at (2.7,-1.9) {7};
\draw[link] (s2.east) -- (c1.west); \draw[link] (c1.east) -- (c2.west);
\node[cell] (d1) at (1.5,-2.85) {18}; \node[cell] (d2) at (2.7,-2.85) {23};
\draw[link] (s3.east) -- (d1.west); \draw[link] (d1.east) -- (d2.west);
\node[font=\scriptsize\itshape,greyline] at (1.7,-3.8) {(empty)};
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

\begin{answerband}
\textbf{Homes:} $10,5\to0$; $36\to1$; $7,12\to2$; $23,18\to3$; nothing $\to4$. Head insertion puts the later key in front, so slot $0$ reads $5\to10$, slot $2$ reads $12\to7$, slot $3$ reads $18\to23$.

\textbf{Load factor} $\alpha=n/m=7/5=\textcolor{violetT}{1.4}$. Under SUHA the expected chain length is $\alpha$, so expected search is $\Theta(1+\alpha)=\textcolor{greenO}{\Theta(1)}$. Keep $m=\Theta(n)$ (resize and rehash when $\alpha$ passes a constant) and all three operations stay expected $\Theta(1)$. \textbf{Worst case:} all keys in one slot, a chain of length $n$, search $\textcolor{rustW}{\Theta(n)}$.
\end{answerband}
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}[Predicted: ``power of two choices'' with chaining]
Two independent hash functions $h_1,h_2$ both satisfy SUHA. When inserting $x$, place it in the \textbf{shorter} of the two chains $T[h_1(x)]$ and $T[h_2(x)]$ (ties go to $h_1$).
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item[(a)] If $k$ of the $m$ slots are empty just before inserting $x$, what is $\Pr[x$ lands in an empty slot$]$?
  \item[(b)] With $m=4$ and current chain lengths $4,1,7,7$ at slots $0,1,2,3$, what is the expected length of the chain $x$ joins (not counting $x$)?
\end{itemize}

\begin{answerband}
\textbf{(a)} $x$ lands in an empty slot exactly when the \emph{shorter} chain is empty, i.e.\ when \textbf{at least one} of the two hashed slots is empty. A single hash misses the empty slots with probability $\tfrac{m-k}{m}$, so
\[
  \Pr[\text{empty}]=1-\Big(\tfrac{m-k}{m}\Big)^{2}.
\]
\textbf{(b)} For any pair of slots $(i,j)$ the joined chain has length $\min(L_i,L_j)$ (a tie joins $h_1$, which is the same length). With $L=(4,1,7,7)$ and all $16$ ordered pairs equally likely,
\[
  \mathbb{E}[\text{length}]=\frac{1}{16}\sum_{i,j}\min(L_i,L_j)=\frac{13+4+19+19}{16}=\frac{55}{16}=\textcolor{violetT}{3.4375}.
\]
Picking the shorter of two random chains pulls the expected length well below the plain $\alpha$.
\end{answerband}
\end{examplebox}

\section*{\textcolor{violetT}{Quick Reference: Lecture 8}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quick Reference: Lecture 8}
\begin{keybox}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.4}
\begin{tabular}{l l}
Direct-access table & $U$ small: store $x$ in $T[k]$; all ops \textcolor{greenO}{$\Theta(1)$}; needs size $|U|$\\
Hash table & $h : U \to \{0,\ldots,m-1\}$; store $x$ in $T[h(k)]$; $m = \Theta(n)$\\
Collision & $h(k_1)=h(k_2)$; unavoidable since $|U| > m$\\
Chaining & linked list per slot; Insert/Delete \textcolor{greenO}{$\Theta(1)$}, Search = chain length\\
Worst-case Search & all keys in one chain $\Rightarrow$ \textcolor{rustW}{$\Theta(n)$}\\
SUHA & $\Pr[h(k)=i] = 1/m$, independent\\
Load factor & $\alpha = n/m = $ expected chain length $E[n_i]$\\
Expected Search & \textcolor{greenO}{$\Theta(1+\alpha)$}; unsuccessful $\alpha$, successful $\alpha/2 + 1$\\
Keep fast & $m = \Theta(n) \Rightarrow \alpha = \Theta(1) \Rightarrow$ Search \textcolor{greenO}{$\Theta(1)$}\\
Resize & keep $\alpha \le c$: $m \ge n/c$; grow $+$ rehash all\\
Division method & $h(k) = k \bmod m$, $m$ prime; avoid $m = 2^{\ell}$\\
Good hash function & uses all bits, spreads keys evenly\\
\end{tabular}
\end{keybox}

\vspace{4pt}
\begin{center}\textcolor{greyline}{\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.6pt}}\end{center}

\newpage

% ==================================================================
% ==================================================================
%  PART IX: LECTURE 9 — BLOOM FILTERS
% ==================================================================
% ==================================================================

\part*{\textcolor{utnavy}{Lecture 9: Bloom Filters}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{Lecture 9: Bloom Filters}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{What is a Bloom Filter?}

\begin{defbox}[\textcolor{defblue}{Bloom Filter (Burton Howard Bloom, 1970)}]
A space-efficient ``\textbf{probabilistic dictionary}''. It keeps the ``fingerprints'' of the elements of a set $S$ (not the elements themselves).

Operations:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item \textcolor{heapblue}{\textbf{BF\_Insert}$(x)$}: $S \leftarrow S \cup \{x\}$.
  \item \textcolor{heapblue}{\textbf{BF\_Search}$(x)$}: returns one of two answers.
\end{itemize}
\end{defbox}

\begin{keybox}[The two answers of BF\_Search]
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.4}
\begin{tabular}{l l}
\textcolor{greenO}{\textbf{``No''}} & $\Rightarrow$ \textbf{definitely} $x \notin S$ \quad (never wrong)\\
\textcolor{rustW}{\textbf{``Probably Yes''}} & $\Rightarrow$ probably $x \in S$, but perhaps not\\
\end{tabular}
\medskip

So a Bloom filter can have \textbf{\textcolor{rustW}{false positives}} (says ``Probably Yes'' for an $x \notin S$), but \textbf{never false negatives}.
\end{keybox}

\begin{examplebox}[Application: malicious URLs]
A browser wants to check if a URL is malicious. The set $S$ of malicious URLs is huge: say $10$ million URLs at $50$ bytes each, so $\approx 500$ MB. We do not want to store that on the user's side.

Store only a \textbf{Bloom filter} of $S$ on the user's side (around $10$ MB). To check a URL:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item \textcolor{greenO}{``No''} $\Rightarrow$ URL $\notin S$, safe to visit the website.
  \item \textcolor{rustW}{``Probably Yes''} $\Rightarrow$ probably malicious, issue a warning.
\end{itemize}
This works with a filter of $\approx 10$ MB and a false positive rate of just $2\%$.
\end{examplebox}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Structure}

\begin{defbox}[Bloom filter structure]
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=2pt]
  \item An array $\mathbf{BF}[0 \ldots m-1]$ of $m$ \textbf{bits}, initially all $0$.
  \item $t$ \textbf{independent} hash functions $h_1, h_2, \ldots, h_t$, each $h_i : U \to \{0, \ldots, m-1\}$, each satisfying SUHA.
\end{itemize}
SUHA here: every element is equally likely to hash to any of the $m$ slots, independent of where the other elements hash.
\end{defbox}

\begin{defbox}[Fingerprint]
The ``\textbf{fingerprint}'' of $x$ is the set of indices $h_1(x), h_2(x), \ldots, h_t(x)$.
\end{defbox}

\begin{codebox}[BF\_Insert(x) and BF\_Search(x)]
\ttfamily BF\_Insert(x):\hfill \textnormal{\itshape // write x's fingerprint into BF}\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{1.5em}for i = 1 to t:\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{3em}BF[h\textsubscript{i}(x)] = 1\\[4pt]
\ttfamily BF\_Search(x):\hfill \textnormal{\itshape // look for x's fingerprint in BF}\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{1.5em}for i = 1 to t:\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{3em}if BF[h\textsubscript{i}(x)] = 0 then return ``No''\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{1.5em}return ``Probably Yes''
\end{codebox}

\begin{keybox}[Why ``No'' is always correct]
If $x$ was ever inserted, \emph{all} its fingerprint bits were set to $1$. So if \emph{any} fingerprint bit of $x$ is still $0$, then $x$ was never inserted: ``No'' is certain. ``Probably Yes'' only means all of $x$'s bits happen to be $1$, which could be due to \textbf{other} elements.
\end{keybox}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Worked Example: $\mathbf{BF}[0 \ldots 7]$ with $t = 2$}

Insert $x_1, x_2, x_3$, then search $x$ and $x'$. Each insert sets two bits; each search reads two bits.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  bit/.style={draw=heapblue, line width=0.7pt, minimum width=0.7cm, minimum height=0.52cm, font=\small, fill=white},
  on/.style={draw=greenO, line width=1pt, minimum width=0.7cm, minimum height=0.52cm, font=\small\bfseries, fill=greenO!15}
]
\node[font=\bfseries] at (0,0.55) {$\mathbf{BF}$};
\node[on]  (b0) at (0,0)    {1};
\node[bit] (b1) at (0,-0.52){0};
\node[bit] (b2) at (0,-1.04){0};
\node[on]  (b3) at (0,-1.56){1};
\node[bit] (b4) at (0,-2.08){0};
\node[on]  (b5) at (0,-2.60){1};
\node[on]  (b6) at (0,-3.12){1};
\node[on]  (b7) at (0,-3.64){1};
\foreach \i/\y in {0/0,1/-0.52,2/-1.04,3/-1.56,4/-2.08,5/-2.60,6/-3.12,7/-3.64}
  {\node[font=\scriptsize\bfseries, rustW, anchor=west] at (0.42,\y) {\i};}
% inserts on left
\node[font=\bfseries, heapblue] at (-4.6,0.55) {INSERTS};
\node[font=\small] at (-4.6,-0.3) {\textbf{BF\_Insert}$(x_1)$: $h_1{=}0,\ h_2{=}3$};
\node[font=\small] at (-4.6,-1.9) {\textbf{BF\_Insert}$(x_2)$: $h_1{=}5,\ h_2{=}6$};
\node[font=\small] at (-4.6,-3.4) {\textbf{BF\_Insert}$(x_3)$: $h_1{=}6,\ h_2{=}7$};
\draw[-{Stealth}, rustW] (-2.9,-0.3) -- (b0.west);
\draw[-{Stealth}, rustW] (-2.9,-0.45) -- (b3.west);
\draw[-{Stealth}, rustW] (-2.9,-1.9) -- (b5.west);
\draw[-{Stealth}, rustW] (-2.9,-2.0) -- (b6.west);
\draw[-{Stealth}, rustW] (-2.9,-3.4) -- (b6.west);
\draw[-{Stealth}, rustW] (-2.9,-3.5) -- (b7.west);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

\begin{examplebox}[Reading two searches]
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=3pt]
  \item \textbf{BF\_Search}$(x)$ with $h_1(x) = 3$, $h_2(x) = 1$: bit $3$ is $1$ but bit $1$ is $\textcolor{greenO}{0}$, so return \textcolor{greenO}{``No''}. Certain: $x \notin \{x_1, x_2, x_3\}$.
  \item \textbf{BF\_Search}$(x')$ with $h_1(x') = 3$, $h_2(x') = 6$: both bits are $1$, so return \textcolor{rustW}{``Probably Yes''}. But $x'$ was never inserted; bit $3$ came from $x_1$ and bit $6$ from $x_2$ and $x_3$. This is a \textbf{\textcolor{rustW}{false positive}}.
\end{itemize}
\end{examplebox}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Probability of a False Positive}

\textbf{Setup.} Insert $x_1, \ldots, x_n$ into an empty $\mathbf{BF}[0 \ldots m-1]$ with $t$ independent hash functions satisfying SUHA. Then do \textbf{BF\_Search}$(x)$ for some $x \notin \{x_1, \ldots, x_n\}$. We want $\Pr[\text{false positive}] = \Pr[\text{BF\_Search}(x) = \text{``Probably Yes''}]$.

\begin{keybox}[Step 1: probability a fixed bit is still $0$]
Fix an index $i$. Across the $n$ inserts, each setting $t$ bits, SUHA and independence of the $h_j$ make the $nt$ ``did not hit $i$'' events mutually independent:
\[
  \Pr[\mathbf{BF}[i] = 0]
  = \prod_{k=1}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{t} \Pr[h_j(x_k) \ne i]
  = \Big(1 - \tfrac{1}{m}\Big)^{nt}
  \;\approx\; \big(e^{-1/m}\big)^{nt}
  = e^{-nt/m},
\]
using $1 - y \approx e^{-y}$ for small $y = 1/m$. Therefore
\[
  \Pr[\mathbf{BF}[i] = 1] = 1 - \Pr[\mathbf{BF}[i]=0] \approx \underbrace{1 - e^{-nt/m}}_{q}.
\]
\end{keybox}

\begin{keybox}[Lemma 1]
After inserting $x_1, \ldots, x_n$ into a $\mathbf{BF}$ of size $m$ with $t$ hash functions, for an arbitrary index $i$:
\[
  \Pr[\mathbf{BF}[i] = 1] \approx 1 - e^{-nt/m} \;=\; q.
\]
\end{keybox}

\begin{keybox}[Step 2: a false positive needs all $t$ fingerprint bits set]
$\text{BF\_Search}(x) = $ ``Probably Yes'' iff $\mathbf{BF}[h_1(x)] = 1$ \textbf{and} $\cdots$ \textbf{and} $\mathbf{BF}[h_t(x)] = 1$. Treating these $t$ bits as independent and each $\approx q$ by Lemma 1:
\[
  \Pr[\text{false positive}]
  \approx q^{\,t}
  = \Big(1 - e^{-nt/m}\Big)^{t}.
\]
\end{keybox}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Choosing $t$: the Optimal Number of Hash Functions}

\begin{keybox}[Optimal $t$]
Let $\dfrac{m}{n}$ = \textbf{number of bits per element} (fix this ratio). Minimise $\Pr[\text{false positive}] = \big(1 - e^{-nt/m}\big)^t$ over $t$ (take the derivative with respect to $t$, set to $0$):
\[
  \text{Optimal } t = (\ln 2)\,\frac{m}{n} = 0.69\,\frac{m}{n},
  \qquad
  \text{then } \Pr[\text{false positive}] \approx 0.62^{\,m/n}.
\]
\end{keybox}

\begin{examplebox}[The malicious-URL numbers]
Want a Bloom filter for $n = 10$ million URLs. Allocate $8$ bits per URL:
\[
  \text{Size } m = 8n = 8 \times 10\text{M bits} \approx 10\text{ MB}, \qquad \frac{m}{n} = 8.
\]
Much less than storing even one full URL per element. The best $t$:
\[
  t \approx 0.69 \cdot \frac{m}{n} = 0.69 \times 8 = 5.52 \text{ hash functions.}
\]
With this $t$:
\[
  \Pr[\text{false positive}] \approx 0.62^{\,m/n} = 0.62^{\,8} \approx \textcolor{rustW}{2\%}.
\]
\end{examplebox}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Past Exam Question}

\begin{exambox}[Seen on a past exam \textnormal{(Final, Dec 2012, Q3: Hash / Bloom Filter)}]
\textbf{[10 marks]} Let $H$ be the family of all hash functions $U \to \{0, \ldots, m-1\}$. For $i = 1, \ldots, k$ let $B_i[0 \ldots m-1]$ be a Boolean array, initially all $0$. Let $S \subseteq U$ with $|S| = n$. Pick $k$ hash functions $h_1, \ldots, h_k$ uniformly and independently from $H$. For each $x \in S$ and each $i$, set $B_i[h_i(x)] \leftarrow 1$. Define
\[
  Check(y) = \textbf{true} \iff \forall i \in \{1,\ldots,k\}\ B_i[h_i(y)] = 1.
\]
\textbf{(a)} For $k=2$, $m=5$, $U = \{0,\ldots,24\}$, $h_1(x) = x \bmod 5$, $h_2(x) = (2x+3)\bmod 5$, $S = \{2,4,7\}$, draw $B_1$ and $B_2$.\\
\textbf{(b)} For $h \in H$ and $x, y \in U$ chosen uniformly and independently, what is $\Pr[h(x) = h(y)]$?\\
\textbf{(c)} If $y \in S$, explain why $Check(y) = \textbf{true}$ always.\\
\textbf{(d)} If $y \notin S$, explain why $\Pr[Check(y) = \textbf{true}] = \big(1 - (1 - 1/m)^n\big)^k$.

\begin{answerband}
This is a Bloom filter with one array per hash function. $Check$ is BF\_Search, and (d) is the false positive probability.

\textbf{(a)} Compute each hash on $S = \{2,4,7\}$:
\[
  h_1:\ 2\mapsto 2,\ 4\mapsto 4,\ 7\mapsto 2 \qquad
  h_2:\ 2\mapsto 2,\ 4\mapsto 1,\ 7\mapsto 2
\]
So $B_1$ has bits $2,4$ set and $B_2$ has bits $1,2$ set:
\[
  B_1 = [\,0,0,\textcolor{greenO}{1},0,\textcolor{greenO}{1}\,], \qquad
  B_2 = [\,0,\textcolor{greenO}{1},\textcolor{greenO}{1},0,0\,]
  \quad (\text{indices } 0..4).
\]

\textbf{(b)} Fix $h(x)$; $h(y)$ is uniform over $m$ slots and independent, so $\Pr[h(x)=h(y)] = \dfrac{1}{m} = \dfrac{1}{5}$.

\textbf{(c)} If $y \in S$, then when $y$ was processed we set $B_i[h_i(y)] \leftarrow 1$ for \emph{every} $i$. Those bits are never reset, so all are still $1$ and $Check(y) = \textbf{true}$. (No false negatives.)

\textbf{(d)} Fix $i$. After inserting the $n$ elements of $S$, by SUHA and independence
\[
  \Pr[B_i[h_i(y)] = 0] = \Big(1 - \tfrac{1}{m}\Big)^{n}
  \;\Rightarrow\;
  \Pr[B_i[h_i(y)] = 1] = 1 - \Big(1 - \tfrac{1}{m}\Big)^{n}.
\]
$Check(y) = \textbf{true}$ needs this for all $k$ independent arrays, so
\[
  \Pr[Check(y) = \textbf{true}] = \Big(1 - (1 - 1/m)^{n}\Big)^{k}. \qquad \textcolor{violetT}{\checkmark}
\]
(Same shape as our $q^t$ with $t = k$.)
\end{answerband}
\end{exambox}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
% ==================================================================
\section{Midterm-Style Process Problems}

\begin{examplebox}[Predicted: insert, query, and estimate the false-positive rate]
A Bloom filter uses an $m=10$ bit array and $t=2$ hash functions. Insert $x$ (hashes to bits $2,5$) and $y$ (hashes to bits $5,8$). Then query $z$ (hashes to bits $2,8$).

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.62, every node/.style={font=\small}]
\foreach \i/\v in {0/0,1/0,2/1,3/0,4/0,5/1,6/0,7/0,8/1,9/0}{
  \ifnum\v=1
    \node[draw=greenO,line width=1pt,fill=greenO!18,minimum size=0.7cm] (c\i) at (\i*0.78,0) {\v};
  \else
    \node[draw=greyline,line width=0.6pt,minimum size=0.7cm] (c\i) at (\i*0.78,0) {\v};
  \fi
  \node[hidx] at (\i*0.78,-0.62) {\i};
}
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}
Does the filter say $z$ is present? Is that correct? Estimate the false-positive probability.

\begin{answerband}
Inserting $x,y$ sets bits $\{2,5,8\}$ (bit $5$ is shared). Querying $z$ checks bits $2$ and $8$, both $1$, so the filter answers \textbf{``probably yes''}. But $z$ was never inserted: this is a \textbf{false positive}. Bloom filters never give false negatives, but another element's bits can cover a query's bits exactly like this.

\textbf{Probability.} A given bit is still $0$ with probability $\big(1-\tfrac1m\big)^{nt}\approx e^{-nt/m}$, so it is $1$ with probability $q=1-e^{-nt/m}$. A false positive needs all $t$ queried bits to be $1$:
\[
  \Pr[\text{false positive}]\approx q^{\,t}=\big(1-e^{-nt/m}\big)^{t}.
\]
Here $n=2,t=2,m=10$: $q=1-e^{-0.4}\approx0.33$, so $\Pr[\text{FP}]\approx 0.33^2\approx\textcolor{violetT}{0.11}$. (The exact finite form uses $1-(1-\tfrac1m)^{nt}$ in place of $q$.)
\end{answerband}
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}[Predicted: size the filter]
You will store $n=10^7$ URLs and want about $8$ bits per URL, so $m=8\times10^7$. What is the optimal number of hash functions $t$, and the resulting false-positive rate?

\begin{answerband}
Optimal $t\approx 0.69\,\tfrac{m}{n}=0.69\times 8\approx\textcolor{violetT}{5.5}$ (use $t=5$ or $6$). The resulting rate is $\Pr[\text{FP}]\approx 0.62^{\,m/n}=0.62^{8}\approx 0.02$, i.e.\ about $2\%$. More bits per element ($m/n$) drives the false-positive rate down exponentially.
\end{answerband}
\end{examplebox}

\section*{\textcolor{violetT}{Quick Reference: Lecture 9}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quick Reference: Lecture 9}
\begin{keybox}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.4}
\begin{tabular}{l l}
Bloom filter & probabilistic dictionary; stores fingerprints, not elements\\
Answers & ``No'' $\Rightarrow$ \textcolor{greenO}{certainly} $x \notin S$; ``Probably Yes'' $\Rightarrow$ maybe (false positives)\\
No false negatives & if $x$ inserted, all its bits are $1$\\
Structure & $m$-bit array $\mathbf{BF}$, $t$ independent hashes $h_i$ under SUHA\\
BF\_Insert$(x)$ & set $\mathbf{BF}[h_i(x)] = 1$ for $i = 1..t$\\
BF\_Search$(x)$ & if any $\mathbf{BF}[h_i(x)] = 0$ return ``No''; else ``Probably Yes''\\
$\Pr[\mathbf{BF}[i]=0]$ & $(1 - 1/m)^{nt} \approx e^{-nt/m}$\\
$\Pr[\mathbf{BF}[i]=1]$ & $q = 1 - e^{-nt/m}$ \quad (Lemma 1)\\
$\Pr[\text{false positive}]$ & $\approx q^t = (1 - e^{-nt/m})^t$\\
Optimal $t$ & $(\ln 2)\,\tfrac{m}{n} = 0.69\,\tfrac{m}{n}$; then $\Pr[\text{FP}] \approx 0.62^{\,m/n}$\\
$m/n$ & bits per element (fix it, then optimise $t$)\\
\end{tabular}
\end{keybox}

% ==================================================================
% ==================================================================
%  PART X: LECTURE 10 — RANDOMIZED QUICKSORT
% ==================================================================
% ==================================================================

\part*{\textcolor{utnavy}{Lecture 10: Randomized Quicksort}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{Lecture 10: Randomized Quicksort}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{The Algorithm}

\begin{defbox}[Randomized Quicksort $RQS(S)$]
Input: a set $S$ of $n$ \textbf{distinct} keys. \quad Output: the keys of $S$ in increasing order.

Recursive, divide-and-conquer:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.6em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item If $S$ is empty, return. If $|S| = 1$, output the key and return.
  \item Select a key $p$ (the \textbf{pivot}) \textcolor{rustW}{uniformly at random} from $S$ (each key equally likely).
  \item Compare $p$ to every other key, splitting $S$ into two subsets:
  \[
    S_< = \{\, s \in S \mid s < p \,\}, \qquad S_> = \{\, s \in S \mid s > p \,\}.
  \]
  (This split costs $n - 1$ key comparisons when $|S| = n$.)
  \item Output $RQS(S_<)$; output $p$; output $RQS(S_>)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{defbox}

\begin{examplebox}[One execution on $S = \{2,8,7,1,3,4,6,5\}$]
Picking pivot $4$ at the root splits into $S_< = \{2,1,3\}$ and $S_> = \{8,7,6,5\}$, then each side recurses with its own random pivots. One possible recursion tree (pivots shown next to each node):

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  every node/.style={ellipse, draw=heapblue, line width=0.6pt, inner sep=2pt, font=\scriptsize, fill=white},
  level distance=1.0cm,
  level 1/.style={sibling distance=4.6cm},
  level 2/.style={sibling distance=2.3cm},
  level 3/.style={sibling distance=1.2cm},
  pv/.style={draw=none, fill=none, font=\scriptsize\bfseries, text=rustW}
]
\node {$2,8,7,1,3,\mathbf{\textcolor{rustW}{4}},6,5$}
  child {node {$2,1,\mathbf{\textcolor{rustW}{3}}$}
    child {node {$2,\mathbf{\textcolor{rustW}{1}}$}
      child {node {$\emptyset$}}
      child {node {$2$}}
    }
    child {node {$\emptyset$}}
  }
  child {node {$8,7,\mathbf{\textcolor{rustW}{6}},5$}
    child {node {$5$}}
    child {node {$\mathbf{\textcolor{rustW}{8}},7$}
      child {node {$7$}}
      child {node {$\emptyset$}}
    }
  };
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}
Output (in-order): $1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8$.
\end{examplebox}

\begin{keybox}[Basic observations (in every $RQS(S)$ execution)]
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=2pt]
  \item Two keys are compared \textbf{only if} one of them is selected as a pivot.
  \item Two keys are compared \textbf{at most once}.
  \item If two keys are ``\textbf{split apart}'' into different subsets by a pivot (like $2$ and $7$ are split by pivot $4$), they are \textbf{never} compared.
\end{itemize}
\end{keybox}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Worst-Case Number of Comparisons}

Fix an input $S$ with $n$ distinct keys, run $RQS(S)$, and let $C$ = number of key comparisons.

\begin{keybox}[Worst case: \textcolor{rustW}{$\Theta(n^2)$}]
If each pivot is the largest (or smallest) remaining key, the split is maximally uneven:
\[
  C = (n-1) + (n-2) + \cdots + 2 + 1 = \frac{n(n-1)}{2}, \quad \text{i.e. } \textcolor{rustW}{\Theta(n^2)}.
\]
\end{keybox}

\textbf{But} the pivot is \emph{random}, so we care about the \textcolor{violetT}{expected} value $E(C)$, averaged over all random pivot choices.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Expected Number of Comparisons: Indicator Variables}

\begin{defbox}[Setup]
Let $z_1 < z_2 < \cdots < z_i < \cdots < z_j < \cdots < z_n$ be the keys of $S$ in \textbf{sorted} order. Define the indicator random variable
\[
  c_{ij} =
  \begin{cases}
    1 & \text{if } RQS(S) \text{ compares } z_i \text{ and } z_j,\\
    0 & \text{otherwise.}
  \end{cases}
\]
Since any pair is compared at most once, the total number of comparisons is
\[
  C = \sum_{1 \le i < j \le n} c_{ij}.
\]
\end{defbox}

\begin{keybox}[Expected value of an indicator = its probability]
\[
  E[c_{ij}] = 1 \cdot \Pr[c_{ij} = 1] + 0 \cdot \Pr[c_{ij} = 0] = \Pr[c_{ij}=1] = \Pr[z_i \text{ and } z_j \text{ are compared}].
\]
By \textbf{linearity of expectation}:
\[
  E(C) = E\!\Big(\sum_{1 \le i < j \le n} c_{ij}\Big)
       = \sum_{1 \le i < j \le n} E[c_{ij}]
       = \sum_{1 \le i < j \le n} \Pr[z_i \text{ and } z_j \text{ are compared}].
\]
\end{keybox}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{The Key Lemma}

\begin{keybox}[Lemma: probability two keys are compared]
For $i < j$:
\[
  \Pr[z_i \text{ and } z_j \text{ are compared}] = \frac{2}{\,j - i + 1\,}.
\]
\end{keybox}

\begin{examplebox}[Intuition on the extremes]
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=2pt]
  \item $z_1$ and $z_n$ (smallest and largest) are compared iff the \textbf{first} pivot chosen among all $n$ keys is $z_1$ or $z_n$: probability $\dfrac{2}{n}$. Here $j - i + 1 = n$, so the lemma checks out. (In the example $n=8$: $\Pr = \tfrac{2}{8} = \tfrac14$.)
  \item $z_i$ and $z_{i+1}$ (adjacent in sorted order) are \textbf{always} compared: nothing lies between them, so whoever becomes a pivot first between the two must compare them. Here $j - i + 1 = 2$, so $\dfrac{2}{2} = 1$. \textcolor{greenO}{\checkmark}
\end{itemize}
\end{examplebox}

\begin{keybox}[Proof of the Lemma]
Consider the set of keys between $z_i$ and $z_j$ inclusive:
\[
  Z_{ij} = \{\, z_i, z_{i+1}, \ldots, z_j \,\}, \qquad |Z_{ij}| = j - i + 1.
\]
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=2pt]
  \item As long as $RQS$ picks pivots \textbf{not in} $Z_{ij}$, all of $Z_{ij}$ stays together inside one subset, and $z_i, z_j$ stay uncompared.
  \item At some point $RQS$ \textbf{must} pick a pivot $p \in Z_{ij}$ (to split $Z_{ij}$). Consider the \textbf{first} such $p$. Two cases:
  \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.6em,itemsep=1pt]
    \item If $z_i < p < z_j$ (so $p$ is a \emph{middle} element of $Z_{ij}$): $p$ splits $z_i$ and $z_j$ apart $\Rightarrow$ they are \textbf{never} compared.
    \item If $p = z_i$ or $p = z_j$: then $z_i$ and $z_j$ \textbf{are} compared.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{itemize}
Since the first pivot in $Z_{ij}$ is equally likely to be any of its $|Z_{ij}| = j-i+1$ keys, and exactly $2$ of them ($z_i$ and $z_j$) cause a comparison:
\[
  \Pr[z_i \text{ and } z_j \text{ compared}] = \Pr[p = z_i \text{ or } p = z_j \mid p \in Z_{ij}] = \frac{2}{\,j - i + 1\,}. \qquad \square
\]
\end{keybox}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Putting it Together: \textcolor{violetT}{$E(C) = O(n \log n)$}}

\begin{keybox}[The bound]
\[
  E(C) = \sum_{1 \le i < j \le n} \frac{2}{\,j - i + 1\,}
       \;\le\; 2n\Big(1 + \tfrac12 + \tfrac13 + \cdots + \tfrac1n\Big)
       \quad\text{[CLRS p.\ 184]}.
\]
The harmonic number $H_n = 1 + \tfrac12 + \cdots + \tfrac1n$ is $O(\log n)$. Therefore
\[
  \boxed{\;E(C) = O(n \log n)\;}.
\]
\end{keybox}

\begin{warnbox}[Worst case vs expected]
Worst case $C = \Theta(n^2)$, but the \textbf{expected} number of comparisons is only $O(n \log n)$. Randomizing the pivot makes the bad $\Theta(n^2)$ inputs vanishingly unlikely: no fixed input is consistently bad.
\end{warnbox}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Past Exam Question}

\begin{exambox}[Seen on a past exam \textnormal{(Final, Aug 2014, Q6: Analysis of a Randomized Algorithm)}]
\textbf{[20 marks]} An array $A$ of $n$ distinct numbers is sorted ascending \textbf{except} that the first two elements $A[1], A[2]$ are swapped (e.g. $[20,10,30,40,\ldots]$). \texttt{RANDOM-SORT} repeatedly picks a pair $(i,j)$ uniformly at random from the set $S$ of all pairs with $1 \le i \le n$, $1 \le j \le n$ (including $i=j$ and $i>j$), removes it, and if $i<j$ and $A[i]>A[j]$ swaps them; it stops once $A$ is sorted.

\textbf{(1)} How many ``while'' iterations are needed to \textbf{guarantee} $A$ is sorted? \textbf{(2)} Expected number of iterations before $A$ is sorted (exact form)?

\begin{answerband}
There are $|S| = n^2$ pairs. The array is sorted the moment the one \textbf{fixing pair} $(1,2)$ is drawn (it is the only pair with $i<j$ and $A[i]>A[j]$); every other pair does nothing.

\textbf{(1)} Worst case: the fixing pair $(1,2)$ is drawn \textbf{last}, after all $n^2 - 1$ useless pairs. So to guarantee sorted we may need all
\[
  \textcolor{violetT}{n^2} \text{ iterations.}
\]

\textbf{(2)} The fixing pair is equally likely to be at any position $1, 2, \ldots, n^2$ in the random removal order. Its expected position is
\[
  E[\text{iterations}] = \frac{1 + 2 + \cdots + n^2}{n^2} = \frac{n^2 + 1}{2}.
\]
\textcolor{violetT}{\textbf{Expected} $= \dfrac{n^2 + 1}{2}$.}

\smallskip
\textcolor{heapblue}{\textbf{Method tested:}} same as $RQS$ analysis: an indicator / uniform-position argument, then expectation.
\end{answerband}
\end{exambox}

\begin{exambox}[Seen on a past exam \textnormal{(Final, Dec 2012, Q8: Randomized Search)}]
\textbf{[15 marks]} A sorted linked list of length $n$ is stored in an $n \times 2$ matrix $A$ (value, next pointer) with a \texttt{head} index. $Search(x)$ first jumps to a \textbf{uniformly random} row $i$, compares $A[i,1]$ with $x$ (resetting to \texttt{head} if too big, else following \texttt{next}), then walks forward. Find the worst-case \textbf{expected} number of comparisons.

\begin{answerband}
\textbf{Worst case (deterministic):} $\Theta(n)$ (a random start near the front, then walk the whole list).

\textbf{Expected:} define indicators over which rows are visited, use linearity of expectation exactly as in the $RQS$ analysis. The random starting row makes the expected walk length a constant fraction of $n$, so the expected number of comparisons is \textcolor{violetT}{$\Theta(n)$} in the worst case, but the \emph{constant} is smaller than the deterministic walk.

\smallskip
\textcolor{heapblue}{\textbf{Why it is here:}} it drills the same toolkit: probability space $+$ indicator random variables $+$ linearity of expectation.
\end{answerband}
\end{exambox}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
% ==================================================================
\section{Midterm-Style Process Problems}

\begin{examplebox}[Predicted: why RQS is $O(n\log n)$ expected]
Let the sorted order be $z_1<z_2<\cdots<z_n$. Show the expected number of key comparisons in randomized quicksort is $O(n\log n)$.

\begin{answerband}
\textbf{Indicators.} Any pair $z_i,z_j$ ($i<j$) is compared \textbf{at most once}, and only if one of them is the \emph{first} pivot chosen from the block $Z_{ij}=\{z_i,\dots,z_j\}$. Let $X_{ij}=1$ if they are ever compared. Then $C=\sum_{i<j}X_{ij}$ and
\[
  \mathbb{E}[C]=\sum_{i<j}\Pr[z_i,z_j\text{ compared}].
\]
\textbf{Lemma.} Each of the $j-i+1$ keys of $Z_{ij}$ is equally likely to be the first pivot; a comparison happens iff that first pivot is $z_i$ or $z_j$ (an interior first pivot splits them apart forever). So
\[
  \Pr[z_i,z_j\text{ compared}]=\frac{2}{\,j-i+1\,}.
\]
\textbf{Sum.} With $d=j-i$,
\[
  \mathbb{E}[C]=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\sum_{d=1}^{n-i}\frac{2}{d+1}<\sum_{i=1}^{n}2H_n=2nH_n=O(n\log n),
\]
where $H_n=\Theta(\log n)$ is the harmonic number.
\end{answerband}
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}[Predicted: worst case and a single pair]
(a) What is the worst-case number of comparisons for RQS, and which pivots cause it? (b) For $n=10$, what is $\Pr[z_2$ and $z_9$ are compared$]$?

\begin{answerband}
\textbf{(a)} The worst case is when every pivot is the current smallest or largest, so each split is of size $0$ and $n-1$. Then the comparison count is $\binom{n}{2}=\tfrac{n(n-1)}{2}=\textcolor{rustW}{\Theta(n^2)}$.

\textbf{(b)} By the lemma, $\Pr=\dfrac{2}{j-i+1}=\dfrac{2}{9-2+1}=\dfrac{2}{8}=\textcolor{violetT}{\tfrac14}$. Far-apart ranks are compared rarely, which is exactly why the expected total collapses to $O(n\log n)$.
\end{answerband}
\end{examplebox}

\section*{\textcolor{violetT}{Quick Reference: Lecture 10}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quick Reference: Lecture 10}
\begin{keybox}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.4}
\begin{tabular}{l l}
$RQS(S)$ & random pivot $p$; split into $S_< , S_>$; recurse; split costs $n-1$\\
Compared when & two keys compared only if one is a pivot; at most once\\
Split apart & a pivot between them $\Rightarrow$ never compared\\
Worst case & $C = \tfrac{n(n-1)}{2} = \Theta(n^2)$\\
Indicator & $c_{ij} = 1$ iff $z_i, z_j$ compared; $C = \sum_{i<j} c_{ij}$\\
$E[c_{ij}]$ & $= \Pr[z_i, z_j \text{ compared}]$ (indicator)\\
Linearity & $E(C) = \sum_{i<j} \Pr[z_i, z_j \text{ compared}]$\\
Key lemma & $\Pr[z_i, z_j \text{ compared}] = \dfrac{2}{j-i+1}$ \; ($Z_{ij}$, first pivot argument)\\
Result & $E(C) \le 2n H_n = \textcolor{violetT}{O(n \log n)}$\\
\end{tabular}
\end{keybox}

\vspace{4pt}
\begin{center}\textcolor{greyline}{\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.6pt}}\end{center}

% ==================================================================

\part*{\textcolor{utnavy}{Tutorial: Augmented Data Structures and Interval Trees}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{Tutorial: Augmented Data Structures \& Interval Trees}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{The Problem: Overlapping Intervals}

\begin{examplebox}[Applications]
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item Book a conference room from $16{:}00$ to $17{:}00$: does it \textbf{conflict} with an existing booking?
  \item Does my lifeguard shift \textbf{overlap} with anyone else's? If so, who?
\end{itemize}
\end{examplebox}

\begin{defbox}[Closed interval and overlap]
A closed interval is $i = [t_1, t_2]$ with $t_1 \le t_2$. Write $\text{low}[i] = t_1$ and $\text{high}[i] = t_2$.

Two intervals $i$ and $i'$ \textbf{overlap} iff:
\[
  \text{low}[i] \le \text{high}[i'] \quad\textbf{and}\quad \text{low}[i'] \le \text{high}[i].
\]
(``Each one starts before the other one ends.'')
\end{defbox}

\begin{defbox}[\textcolor{defblue}{Goal}]
Store a set of intervals and support, all in \textcolor{greenO}{$O(\log n)$} time ($n$ = number of intervals):
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item \textbf{Insert}$(x)$, \textbf{Delete}$(x)$,
  \item \textbf{Search}$(x)$: return \emph{an} interval in the structure that overlaps with $x$ (or \texttt{NIL}).
\end{itemize}
\end{defbox}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Designing an Augmented Data Structure (the Method)}

\begin{keybox}[The design steps (this method is the important part)]
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.6em,itemsep=3pt]
  \item \textbf{What base data structure, and \textcolor{rustW}{why}?}
  \item \textbf{What information should be stored?}
    \begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
      \item the \textbf{key} of the base structure,
      \item the \textbf{augmentations} (extra fields).
    \end{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Maintain the augmentations efficiently}: each must be computable in $O(1)$ from a node's own fields and its children's fields.
  \item Implement the new operation using the augmentations.
\end{enumerate}
\end{keybox}

\begin{keybox}[Step 1 and 2 for interval trees]
\textbf{Base structure: an AVL tree.} Why? It gives \textbf{ordering} plus $O(\log n)$-height operations.

For each node $x$ storing interval $i = (\text{low}[i], \text{high}[i])$:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=2pt]
  \item \textbf{Key} $= \text{low}[x]$ (the low endpoint). The AVL is ordered by low endpoints.
  \item \textbf{Augmentation} $\max[x] = $ the largest high endpoint among all intervals in $x$'s subtree.
\end{itemize}
\end{keybox}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Maintaining the Augmentation \texorpdfstring{$\max[x]$}{max[x]} Efficiently}

\begin{keybox}[{$\max[x]$ is computable in \textcolor{greenO}{$O(1)$} from the children}]
\[
  \max[x] = \max\big(\,\text{high}[x],\; \max[x.\text{left}],\; \max[x.\text{right}]\,\big).
\]
So after any structural change, $\max$ only needs updating on the $O(\log n)$ nodes on the path from the root to the modified leaf, plus the $O(1)$ rotated nodes.
\end{keybox}

\begin{keybox}[Inserts and Deletes stay \textcolor{greenO}{$O(\log n)$}]
Insert / Delete $=$ ordinary AVL insert / delete $+$ $\max$ updates. The updates apply only to nodes on the path from the root to the modified leaf, plus rotated nodes:
\[
  O(\log n) \text{ nodes} \times O(1) \text{ update each} = \textcolor{greenO}{O(\log n)}.
\]
\end{keybox}

\begin{examplebox}[A small interval tree (keyed on low, with $\max$ below each node)]
\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  every node/.style={draw=heapblue, line width=0.7pt, rounded corners=2pt, inner sep=2.5pt, font=\scriptsize, fill=white},
  level distance=1.35cm,
  level 1/.style={sibling distance=4.2cm},
  level 2/.style={sibling distance=2.1cm},
  mx/.style={draw=none, fill=none, font=\scriptsize\bfseries, text=rustW}
]
\node {$[12.5,\,13]$}
  child {node {$[10,\,12]$}
    child {node {$[9,\,9.5]$}}
    child[missing]
  }
  child {node {$[14.5,\,19.5]$}
    child {node {$[14,\,15]$}}
    child[missing]
  };
% max labels
\node[mx] at (0,-0.55) {$\max = 19.5$};
\node[mx] at (-2.1,-1.9) {$\max = 12$};
\node[mx] at (2.1,-1.9) {$\max = 19.5$};
\node[mx] at (-3.15,-3.25) {$\max = 9.5$};
\node[mx] at (1.05,-3.25) {$\max = 15$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}
Each $\max$ equals the largest high endpoint in that node's subtree, and each is the max of the node's own high and its children's $\max$ values.
\end{examplebox}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Interval-Search$(x)$}

\begin{codebox}[Search(x): find any interval overlapping x]
\ttfamily y = root\\
\ttfamily while y != NULL and (y and x do NOT overlap):\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{2em}if y.left = NULL or max[y.left] < low[x]:\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{4em}y = y.right\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{2em}else:\\
\ttfamily \hspace*{4em}y = y.left\\
\ttfamily return y
\end{codebox}

\textbf{Cost:} one root-to-leaf descent, $O(1)$ per node, so \textcolor{greenO}{$O(\log n)$}.

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Why it is Correct}

\begin{keybox}[Loop invariant]
\textbf{If} there is an interval that overlaps with the query $x$, \textbf{then} there always is one such interval in $y$'s subtree.
\end{keybox}

\begin{keybox}[Maintaining the invariant]
\textbf{Holds at the root} (the whole tree is $y$'s subtree).

\textbf{After each loop iteration}, when $y$ and $x$ do not overlap we move to a child:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.6em,itemsep=2pt]
  \item[(i)] If $y.\text{left} = \texttt{NULL}$: the left subtree is empty, so any overlapping interval must be on the right. Go right. \textcolor{greenO}{\checkmark}
  \item[(ii)] If $\max[y.\text{left}] < \text{low}[x]$: every interval in the left subtree ends before $x$ starts, so none can overlap $x$. Go right. \textcolor{greenO}{\checkmark}
  \item[(iii)] If $\max[y.\text{left}] \ge \text{low}[x]$: some interval in the left subtree ends at or after $\text{low}[x]$. One can show that if \emph{any} interval in $y$'s subtree overlaps $x$, then one in the \textbf{left} subtree does too, so going left is safe. Go left. \textcolor{greenO}{\checkmark}
\end{enumerate}
When the loop ends, either $y = \texttt{NULL}$ (no overlap exists, by the invariant) or $y$ overlaps $x$.
\end{keybox}

\begin{examplebox}[A worked search]
Interval tree (key $=$ low, $\max$ below each node):
\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  every node/.style={draw=heapblue, line width=0.7pt, rounded corners=2pt, inner sep=2.5pt, font=\scriptsize, fill=white},
  level distance=1.35cm,
  level 1/.style={sibling distance=4.6cm},
  level 2/.style={sibling distance=2.3cm},
  mx/.style={draw=none, fill=none, font=\scriptsize\bfseries, text=rustW}
]
\node {$[5,\,10]$}
  child {node {$[3,\,9]$}
    child {node {$[2,\,4]$}}
    child {node {$[4,\,5]$}}
  }
  child {node {$[8,\,11]$}
    child {node {$[7,\,30]$}}
    child {node {$[10,\,20]$}}
  };
\node[mx] at (0,-0.55) {$30$};
\node[mx] at (-2.3,-1.9) {$9$};
\node[mx] at (2.3,-1.9) {$30$};
\node[mx] at (-3.45,-3.25) {$4$};
\node[mx] at (-1.15,-3.25) {$5$};
\node[mx] at (1.15,-3.25) {$30$};
\node[mx] at (3.45,-3.25) {$20$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

\textbf{Search $x = [12, 13]$:}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item At $[5,10]$: no overlap ($10 < 12$). $\max[\text{left}] = 9 < \text{low}[x] = 12$, so go \textbf{right}.
  \item At $[8,11]$: no overlap ($11 < 12$). $\max[\text{left}] = 30 \ge 12$, so go \textbf{left}.
  \item At $[7,30]$: overlap? $\text{low} = 7 \le 13$ and $12 \le 30$: \textcolor{greenO}{yes}. Return $[7,30]$.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Search $x = [0, 1]$:} at $[5,10]$ no overlap, $\max[\text{left}] = 9 \ge 0$ so go left; at $[3,9]$ no overlap, $\max[\text{left}] = 4 \ge 0$ so go left; at $[2,4]$ no overlap, left is \texttt{NULL} so go right; right is \texttt{NULL}, loop ends with $y = \texttt{NULL}$. Correctly reports \textbf{no overlapping interval}.
\end{examplebox}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Past Exam Questions}

\begin{exambox}[Seen on a past exam \textnormal{(Final, Dec 2012, Q2: BST / Augmentation)}]
\textbf{[10 marks]} Define $Average(v)$: the average of all keys in the subtree rooted at node $v$. Augment an AVL tree to support $Average$ in $O(1)$ without hurting the $O(\log n)$ bound of Insert and Delete.

\textbf{(a)} What extra info at each node? \textbf{(b)} How to do $Average$ in $O(1)$? \textbf{(c)} Why do Insert/Delete stay $O(\log n)$?

\begin{answerband}
\textbf{(a)} Store at each node $v$ two augmentations:
\[
  \text{size}[v] = \text{number of nodes in } v\text{'s subtree}, \qquad
  \text{sum}[v] = \text{sum of all keys in } v\text{'s subtree}.
\]

\textbf{(b)} $Average(v) = \dfrac{\text{sum}[v]}{\text{size}[v]}$, which is $\textcolor{greenO}{O(1)}$.

\textbf{(c)} Both augmentations are computable in $O(1)$ from the children:
\[
  \text{size}[v] = 1 + \text{size}[v.\text{left}] + \text{size}[v.\text{right}], \quad
  \text{sum}[v] = \text{key}[v] + \text{sum}[v.\text{left}] + \text{sum}[v.\text{right}].
\]
They only change on the $O(\log n)$ nodes on the insert/delete path plus the $O(1)$ rotated nodes, each updated in $O(1)$: total still \textcolor{greenO}{$O(\log n)$}. This is the exact same augmentation method as $\max[x]$ for interval trees.
\end{answerband}
\end{exambox}

\begin{exambox}[Seen on a past exam \textnormal{(Final, Dec 2013, Q10: Range Search)}]
\textbf{[15 marks]} Support on a set $S$ of integers, each in $O(\log n)$: \texttt{Insert}$(k)$, \texttt{Delete}$(x)$, \texttt{Search}$(k)$, and
\[
  IsEmptyRange(S, a, b) = \textbf{true} \iff S \text{ has no integer in } [a,b].
\]
\textbf{(a)} Which structure (no modifications needed)? \textbf{(b)} Pseudocode for $IsEmptyRange$. \textbf{(c)} Why $O(\log n)$?

\begin{answerband}
\textbf{(a)} A \textbf{balanced BST (AVL tree)} keyed on the integers. Insert, Delete, Search are already $O(\log n)$.

\textbf{(b)} Find the smallest key that is $\ge a$ (the \emph{successor} of $a-1$), then check whether it falls in $[a,b]$:
\begin{quote}\ttfamily
IsEmptyRange(S, a, b):\\
\hspace*{1.5em}k = smallest key in S with k $\ge$ a\\
\hspace*{1.5em}\hspace*{1.5em}// walk down: at node x, if x.key $\ge$ a go left (remember x), else go right\\
\hspace*{1.5em}if k = NIL or k $>$ b: return true\\
\hspace*{1.5em}else: return false
\end{quote}

\textbf{(c)} Finding the smallest key $\ge a$ is a single root-to-leaf descent, $O(1)$ per node, on a tree of height $O(\log n)$: so $O(\log n)$. If that key exists and is $\le b$, there is an integer of $S$ in $[a,b]$; otherwise the range is empty.

\smallskip
\textcolor{violetT}{\textbf{Link to interval trees:}} same idea, a balanced BST answering a \emph{range} question in $O(\log n)$ by using ordering (here no augmentation is even needed).
\end{answerband}
\end{exambox}

% ------------------------------------------------------------------
% ==================================================================
\section{Midterm-Style Process Problems}

\begin{examplebox}[Predicted: fill max fields, then run Interval-Search]
Each node shows $[\text{low},\text{high}]$. Fill $\text{max}[x]$ at every node, then run \textsc{Interval-Search} for $i=[21,23]$.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
   level distance=1.5cm,
   every node/.style={rectangle, rounded corners=2pt, draw=heapblue, line width=0.7pt, minimum width=1.2cm, minimum height=6mm, inner sep=2pt, font=\scriptsize, fill=white},
   level 1/.style={sibling distance=4.2cm}, level 2/.style={sibling distance=2.2cm}
]
\node {$[15,20]$}
  child {node {$[10,30]$} child {node {$[5,8]$}} child {node {$[12,16]$}} }
  child {node {$[25,35]$} child {node {$[17,19]$}} child[missing] };
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

\begin{answerband}
\textbf{Max fields}, bottom up, $\text{max}=\max$ of own high and children's max: $[5,8]\!\to\!8$, $[12,16]\!\to\!16$, $[17,19]\!\to\!19$, $[10,30]\!\to\!\max(30,8,16)=\textcolor{violetT}{30}$, $[25,35]\!\to\!\max(35,19)=\textcolor{violetT}{35}$, root $[15,20]\!\to\!\max(20,30,35)=\textcolor{violetT}{35}$.

\textbf{Search $[21,23]$} (go left iff left child exists and $\text{max}[\text{left}]\geq\text{low}[i]=21$, else right):
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.6em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item At $[15,20]$: overlap needs $15\leq23$ and $21\leq20$; the second fails, \textbf{no overlap}. $\text{max}[\text{left}]=30\geq21$, so \textcolor{heapblue}{go left}.
  \item At $[10,30]$: $10\leq23$ and $21\leq30$, both hold. \textcolor{greenO}{\textbf{Overlap, return }$[10,30]$.}
\end{enumerate}
\end{answerband}
\end{examplebox}

\begin{exambox}[Find-the-bug \textnormal{(modern MC style)}]
\begin{quote}\ttfamily
Buggy-Interval-Search(T, i)\\
1\ \ x = T.root\\
2\ \ while x $\ne$ nil and i does not overlap x.int:\\
3\ \ \ \ \ if x.left $\ne$ nil and x.left.max $<$ i.low:\\
4\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ x = x.left\\
5\ \ \ \ \ else x = x.right\\
6\ \ return x
\end{quote}
Which line contains the bug? \quad (a) line 2 \quad (b) line 3 \quad (c) lines 4 and 5 \quad (d) line 6

\begin{answerband}
\textbf{(b) line 3.} The test is backwards. Go \textbf{left} when the left subtree \emph{could} hold an overlap, i.e.\ when $\text{x.left.max}\geq\text{i.low}$. As written it goes left exactly when the left subtree cannot help, so it misses overlaps on the left. Fix: \texttt{x.left.max $\geq$ i.low}.
\end{answerband}
\end{exambox}

\section*{\textcolor{violetT}{Quick Reference: Interval Trees}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quick Reference: Interval Trees}
\begin{keybox}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.4}
\begin{tabular}{l l}
Overlap test & $\text{low}[i] \le \text{high}[i']$ \textbf{and} $\text{low}[i'] \le \text{high}[i]$\\
Design steps & base DS (+ why) $\to$ key $+$ augmentations $\to$ maintain in $O(1)$ $\to$ use\\
Base structure & AVL (ordering $+$ $O(\log n)$)\\
Key & $\text{low}[x]$ (low endpoint)\\
Augmentation & $\max[x] = $ largest high endpoint in $x$'s subtree\\
Maintain $\max$ & $\max[x] = \max(\text{high}[x], \max[x.\text{left}], \max[x.\text{right}])$; $O(1)$ per node\\
Insert / Delete & AVL $+$ $\max$ updates on path $+$ rotated nodes $= O(\log n)$\\
Search$(x)$ & go right if left empty or $\max[\text{left}] < \text{low}[x]$, else go left; $O(\log n)$\\
Invariant & if any interval overlaps $x$, one such interval is in $y$'s subtree\\
General method & store size/sum/max etc. computable in $O(1)$ from children\\
\end{tabular}
\end{keybox}

\vspace{4pt}
\begin{center}\textcolor{greyline}{\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.6pt}}\end{center}
\begin{center}\small\textcolor{greyline}{End of Lectures 8, 9, 10 \& Interval Trees tutorial notes.}\end{center}

% ******************************************************************
%  LECTURE 11 -- DISJOINT SETS (UNION/FIND): LISTS, FORESTS, WEIGHTED UNION
% ******************************************************************

\part*{\textcolor{utnavy}{Lecture 11: Disjoint Sets (Union--Find), Part 1}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{Lecture 11: Disjoint Sets (Union--Find), Part 1}
\setcounter{section}{0}

% ==================================================================
\section{The Disjoint Sets ADT}

\begin{defbox}[Disjoint Sets (Union--Find) ADT]
\textbf{Object:} $n$ distinct elements named $1, 2, \ldots, n$. Initially each element is in its own \textcolor{rustW}{singleton set}:
\[
  S_1 = \{1\},\quad S_2 = \{2\},\quad \ldots,\quad S_n = \{n\}.
\]
Each set has a \textcolor{rustW}{representative} element that uniquely identifies it. Write $S_x$ for the set represented by $x$.

\textbf{Operations:}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item \textbf{Union}$(x,y)$: here $x, y$ are \underline{distinct set representatives}. Replace $S_x$ and $S_y$ by $S = S_x \cup S_y$. Some element of $S$ becomes the representative of $S$.
  \item \textbf{Find}$(z)$: return the representative of the set that contains $z$.
\end{itemize}
\end{defbox}

\begin{keybox}[The sequence $\sigma$ we care about]
Each \textbf{Union} merges two sets into one, so it \textcolor{rustW}{reduces the number of sets by 1}. Starting from $n$ singletons, there can be \textcolor{rustW}{at most $n-1$ Unions}.
\[
  \sigma \;=\; \text{a sequence of } (n-1)\ \textbf{Unions} \text{ mixed with } m \ge n\ \textbf{Finds}.
\]
\textbf{Goal:} a data structure that minimizes the \textcolor{rustW}{total cost} of executing such a sequence $\sigma$.
\end{keybox}

\begin{examplebox}[Tracing the operations ($S_j$ = set represented by $j$)]
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.3}
\begin{tabular}{l l}
Initially & $S_1{=}\{\underline{1}\}$, \; $S_2{=}\{\underline{2}\}$, \; $S_3{=}\{\underline{3}\}$, \; $S_4{=}\{\underline{4}\}$, \; $S_5{=}\{\underline{5}\}$\\
\textbf{Union}$(3,4)$ & $S_3 = \{\underline{3},4\}$ \quad (now $\mathbf{Find}(4)=3$)\\
\textbf{Union}$(1,5)$ & $S_1 = \{\underline{1},5\}$\\
\textbf{Union}$(1,3)$ & $S_1 = \{\underline{1},5,3,4\}$ \quad (now $\mathbf{Find}(4)=1$, \; $\mathbf{Find}(2)=2$)\\
\textbf{Union}$(1,2)$ & $S_1 = \{\underline{1},5,3,4,2\}$\\
\end{tabular}
\end{examplebox}

% ==================================================================
\section{Implementation 1: Linked Lists}

\begin{defbox}[One linked list per set]
Store each set as a doubly linked list, with a \textbf{head} and \textbf{tail} pointer. The \textcolor{rustW}{first element of the list is the representative}.
\end{defbox}

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.9,
  box/.style={draw=heapblue,line width=0.7pt,minimum size=0.7cm,inner sep=2pt,font=\small\bfseries,text=rustW},
  lk/.style={heapblue,line width=0.8pt,{Stealth}-{Stealth}}]
\node[box] (a) at (0,0) {1}; \node[box] (b) at (1.5,0) {5};
\node[box] (c) at (3,0) {3}; \node[box] (d) at (4.5,0) {4};
\draw[lk] (a)--(b); \draw[lk] (b)--(c); \draw[lk] (c)--(d);
\node[rustW,font=\small] at (-0.9,0.55) {$S_1$};
\node[rustW,font=\scriptsize] at (-0.9,0.0) {$h_1\!\downarrow$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

\begin{keybox}[Costs]
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.3}
\begin{tabular}{l l}
\textbf{Union}$(x,y)$ & splice tail of one list onto head of the other $\to$ \textcolor{greenO}{$O(1)$}\\
\textbf{Find}$(z)$ & walk from $z$ to the head to read the representative $\to$ \textcolor{rustW}{$O(n)$}\\
\end{tabular}
\[
  \text{Worst-case cost of } \sigma \;=\; O(\underbrace{n\cdot 1}_{\text{per Union}} + \underbrace{m \cdot n}_{\text{per Find}}) \;=\; \textcolor{rustW}{O(m\,n)}.
\]
\end{keybox}

% ==================================================================
\section{Implementation 2: Augmented Linked Lists}

\begin{defbox}[Augmented linked list]
Same as before, but \textbf{each element also stores a pointer to its representative}. Now \textbf{Find} reads that pointer directly.
\end{defbox}

\begin{keybox}[Costs flip]
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.3}
\begin{tabular}{l l}
\textbf{Find}$(z)$ & follow the representative pointer $\to$ \textcolor{greenO}{$O(1)$}\\
\textbf{Union}$(x,y)$ & concatenate, then \textcolor{rustW}{redirect every element's pointer} of one set $\to$ \textcolor{rustW}{$O(n)$}\\
\end{tabular}
\[
  \text{Worst-case cost of } \sigma \;=\; O(\underbrace{n\cdot n}_{\text{per Union}} + \underbrace{m \cdot 1}_{\text{per Find}}) \;=\; \textcolor{rustW}{O(n^2 + m)}.
\]
\end{keybox}

\subsection{Weighted Union (WU) on augmented lists}

\begin{keybox}[WU rule (by size)]
When merging, \textbf{append the smaller list onto the bigger list} (keep a size counter for each list). This way we redirect the \textcolor{greenO}{fewer} pointers.
\end{keybox}

Each \textbf{Union} is still $O(n)$ in the worst case and each \textbf{Find} is $O(1)$, but WU improves the \emph{total} cost of a whole sequence:

\begin{keybox}[Claim (proved in tutorial)]
With WU, the worst-case cost of executing $\sigma$ is
\[
  O(\underbrace{m}_{m\ \text{Finds}} + \underbrace{n\log n}_{(n-1)\ \text{Unions}}).
\]
\end{keybox}

% ==================================================================
\section{Implementation 3: Disjoint-Set Forest}

\begin{defbox}[Forest representation]
Each set is a \textcolor{rustW}{tree}:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item each node holds one element,
  \item each \textbf{non-root} node points to its \textbf{parent},
  \item the \textbf{root} holds the set representative.
\end{itemize}
An array $A[1..n]$ stores, for each element $x$, a pointer $A[x]$ to $x$'s node, so we can jump to any element in $O(1)$.
\end{defbox}

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.9,
  fn/.style={circle,draw=heapblue,line width=0.8pt,minimum size=0.72cm,inner sep=0pt,font=\small\bfseries,text=rustW,fill=white},
  up/.style={heapblue,line width=0.8pt,-{Stealth}}]
% tree for S1 = {1,3,2,8,5,10}, root 1
\node[fn,draw=rustW,line width=1.1pt] (n1) at (1.2,2.4) {1};
\node[fn] (n3) at (0,1.2) {3};
\node[fn] (n8) at (2.4,1.2) {8};
\node[fn] (n2) at (1.4,0) {2};
\node[fn] (n5) at (2.4,0) {5};
\node[fn] (n10) at (3.4,0) {10};
\draw[up] (n3)--(n1); \draw[up] (n8)--(n1);
\draw[up] (n2)--(n8); \draw[up] (n5)--(n8); \draw[up] (n10)--(n8);
\node[rustW,font=\small] at (1.2,3.05) {$S_1=\{\underline{1},3,2,8,5,10\}$};
% second tree for S9
\node[fn,draw=rustW,line width=1.1pt] (m9) at (6.3,2.4) {9};
\node[fn] (m7) at (5.5,1.2) {7};
\node[fn] (m4) at (7.1,1.2) {4};
\draw[up] (m7)--(m9); \draw[up] (m4)--(m9);
\node[rustW,font=\small] at (6.3,3.05) {$S_9=\{\underline{9},7,4\}$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

\begin{keybox}[Costs]
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.35}
\begin{tabular}{l l}
\textbf{Find}$(x)$ & follow parent pointers up to the root \;$\to$\; \textcolor{violetT}{$O(1 + \text{Find-path length})$}\\
\textbf{Union}$(x,y)$ & make root of $S_x$ a child of root of $S_y$ \;$\to$\; \textcolor{greenO}{$O(1)$}\\
\end{tabular}
\end{keybox}

\begin{warnbox}[Without any heuristic, the forest can degenerate]
Do \textbf{Union}$(1,2)$, \textbf{Union}$(2,3)$, $\ldots$, \textbf{Union}$(n{-}1,n)$ (each time attaching the old root under the new element). This builds a single chain of \textbf{height $n-1$}.
\[
  \text{Cost of } m\ \textbf{Find}(1) \;=\; \Omega(m\cdot n)
  \quad\Rightarrow\quad \text{worst-case } \sigma = \Omega(m\,n).
\]
To reduce the cost of $\sigma$, we must \textcolor{rustW}{reduce the length of Find paths}, i.e.\ reduce the \textbf{height} of the trees formed during $\sigma$.
\end{warnbox}

% ==================================================================
\section{Heuristic 1: Weighted Union (WU) by Size}

\begin{defbox}[WU rule (MUST KNOW)]
In \textbf{Union}$(A,B)$, make the root of the \textcolor{rustW}{smaller} tree a child of the root of the \textcolor{greenO}{bigger} tree. Here \textbf{size} = number of nodes in the tree. Store a size counter at each root.
\end{defbox}

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.95,
  rt/.style={circle,draw=heapblue,line width=0.9pt,minimum size=0.55cm,inner sep=0pt,font=\small\bfseries,text=rustW,fill=white}]
% small tree A
\node[rt] (A) at (0,0) {A};
\draw[heapblue,line width=0.7pt] (-0.55,-1.1)--(A)--(0.55,-1.1)--(-0.55,-1.1);
\node[font=\scriptsize] at (0,-0.75) {smaller};
% big tree B
\node[rt] (B) at (3,0.5) {B};
\draw[heapblue,line width=0.7pt] (2.1,-1.1)--(B)--(3.9,-1.1)--(2.1,-1.1);
\node[font=\scriptsize] at (3,-0.55) {bigger};
\draw[-{Stealth},heapblue,line width=1pt] (A)--(B);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

\begin{keybox}[Lemma (the key fact)]
With WU, any tree $T$ of height $h$ created while running $\sigma$ has at least $2^h$ nodes:
\[
  |T| \;\ge\; 2^{\,h}.
\]
\end{keybox}

\begin{examplebox}[Proof sketch: induction on $h$]
\textbf{Base} $h=0$: a single node, $|T| = 1 = 2^0$. \checkmark

\textbf{Step:} assume the lemma for height $h$. A tree of height $h+1$ is formed by a \textbf{Union} where the smaller tree $A$ (whose root sits at depth, giving height $h$) is hung under the bigger tree $B$:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item By I.H., $|A| \ge 2^h$ (the subtree that reached height $h$).
  \item By WU, $B$ is the bigger tree, so $|B| \ge |A| \ge 2^h$.
\end{itemize}
Therefore $|T| = |A| + |B| \ge 2^h + 2^h = 2^{\,h+1}$. \checkmark
\end{examplebox}

\begin{keybox}[Corollary and Theorem]
Since $|T| \le n$, we get $2^{\,h} \le |T| \le n$, so
\[
  h \;\le\; \textcolor{greenO}{\log_2 n}.
\]
\textbf{Theorem.} With WU, executing any sequence $\sigma$ of $n-1$ Unions and $m \ge n$ Finds takes
\[
  O(m + n\log n) \;=\; \textcolor{violetT}{O(m\log n)} \qquad (\text{since } m \ge n).
\]
\end{keybox}

% ==================================================================
\section{Past Exam Questions}

\begin{exambox}[Seen on a past exam \textnormal{(Dec 2019 Final, MCQ)}]
Linked-list disjoint sets where every node stores a back-pointer to the representative.

\textbf{Q12.} Worst-case cost of a single \texttt{Union}$(x,y)$ / \texttt{MakeSet}$(x)$ / \texttt{FindSet}$(x)$?

\textbf{Q13.} Using union-by-weight, worst-case cost of a sequence of $n$ operations (\texttt{MakeSet}, \texttt{FindSet}, \texttt{Union})?

\begin{answerband}
\textbf{Q12.} $\textcolor{rustW}{O(n)},\ \textcolor{greenO}{O(1)},\ \textcolor{greenO}{O(1)}$. A back-pointer makes \texttt{FindSet} and \texttt{MakeSet} $O(1)$, but \texttt{Union} must redirect every back-pointer of one set, so $O(n)$. (This is exactly our augmented-linked-list implementation.)

\smallskip
\textbf{Q13.} $\textcolor{violetT}{O(n\log n)}$. This is the weighted-union bound: a whole sequence costs $O(m + n\log n)$, which for $n$ total operations is $O(n\log n)$.
\end{answerband}
\end{exambox}

\begin{exambox}[Seen on a past exam \textnormal{(Dec 2019 Final, MCQ)}]
\textbf{Q24.} \texttt{UNKNOWN} operates on a disjoint-set forest. Identify it:
\begin{quote}\ttfamily
UNKNOWN(x):\\
\hspace*{1.5em}if x $\ne$ x.p:\\
\hspace*{3em}x.p = UNKNOWN(x.p)\\
\hspace*{1.5em}return x.p
\end{quote}
\textbf{Q23.} Kruskal's algorithm employs which data structure?

\begin{answerband}
\textbf{Q24.} \textcolor{violetT}{Find-Set with path compression.} It walks up to the root and, on the way back, re-points every node on the path straight to the root (next lecture).

\smallskip
\textbf{Q23.} A \textcolor{violetT}{disjoint set} (Union--Find), used to detect whether adding an edge would form a cycle.
\end{answerband}
\end{exambox}

\section{Midterm-Style Process Problems}

\begin{examplebox}[Predicted: run a sequence of Weighted Union operations]
Start with \textsc{MakeSet}$(1),\dots,\textsc{MakeSet}(6)$. Apply, using \textbf{weighted union by size} (hang the smaller tree's root under the larger; on a tie the first argument becomes the root): \textsc{Union}$(1,2)$, \textsc{Union}$(3,4)$, \textsc{Union}$(5,6)$, \textsc{Union}$(1,3)$, \textsc{Union}$(1,5)$. Draw the final forest.

\begin{answerband}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item \textsc{Union}$(1,2),(3,4),(5,6)$: three size-$2$ trees rooted at $1$, $3$, $5$ (each tie makes the first arg the root).
  \item \textsc{Union}$(1,3)$: both size $2$, tie, so root $1$; $3$ (with its child $4$) hangs under $1$.
  \item \textsc{Union}$(1,5)$: sizes $4$ vs $2$, so the smaller set (rooted $5$) hangs under $1$.
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
   every node/.style={circle, draw=heapblue, line width=0.7pt, minimum size=7mm, inner sep=1pt, font=\small, fill=white},
   edge/.style={-{Stealth},heapblue,line width=0.7pt}
]
\node[draw=violetT,line width=1pt] (n1) at (0,0) {1};
\node (n2) at (-1.6,-1.2) {2};
\node (n3) at (0,-1.2) {3};
\node (n5) at (1.6,-1.2) {5};
\node (n4) at (0,-2.4) {4};
\node (n6) at (1.6,-2.4) {6};
\draw[edge] (n2)--(n1); \draw[edge] (n3)--(n1); \draw[edge] (n5)--(n1);
\draw[edge] (n4)--(n3); \draw[edge] (n6)--(n5);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}
Root $1$ holds all $6$ elements. Weighted union keeps the height at $O(\log n)$ (a tree of height $h$ needs $\geq 2^{h}$ nodes), so each \textsc{FindSet} is $O(\log n)$ and a sequence of $m$ operations costs \textcolor{violetT}{$O(m\log n)$}.
\end{answerband}
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}[Predicted: which implementation for which cost]
Fill the worst-case single-operation cost for each disjoint-set implementation.

\begin{answerband}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.3}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{l c c c}
 & \textsc{MakeSet} & \textsc{FindSet} & \textsc{Union}\\
\hline
Linked list (with rep pointer) & $O(1)$ & $O(1)$ & $O(n)$ (relabel the smaller list)\\
Forest, no heuristics & $O(1)$ & $O(n)$ (tall chain) & $O(n)$\\
Forest, weighted union & $O(1)$ & $O(\log n)$ & $O(\log n)$\\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The forest with weighted union is the one worth memorizing: it turns the degenerate $O(n)$ chain into $O(\log n)$ height.
\end{answerband}
\end{examplebox}

\section*{\textcolor{violetT}{Quick Reference: Lecture 11}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quick Reference: Lecture 11}
\begin{keybox}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.35}
\begin{tabular}{l l}
ADT & $n$ elements, singletons; rep.\ per set; \textbf{Union}(distinct reps), \textbf{Find}$(z)$\\
$\sigma$ & $n-1$ Unions mixed with $m \ge n$ Finds; minimize total cost\\
Linked list & Union $O(1)$, Find $O(n)$ \;$\to$\; $\sigma = O(mn)$\\
Augmented list & Find $O(1)$, Union $O(n)$ \;$\to$\; $\sigma = O(n^2 + m)$\\
WU on lists & append smaller onto bigger \;$\to$\; $\sigma = O(m + n\log n)$\\
Forest & non-root $\to$ parent, root $=$ rep; Find $O(1+\text{path})$, Union $O(1)$\\
No heuristic & chain of height $n-1$ \;$\to$\; $\sigma = \Omega(mn)$\\
WU by size & smaller tree becomes child of bigger\\
WU lemma & height $h$ tree has $\ge 2^h$ nodes $\Rightarrow h \le \log_2 n$\\
WU theorem & $\sigma$ takes \textcolor{violetT}{$O(m\log n)$}\\
\end{tabular}
\end{keybox}

\part*{\textcolor{utnavy}{Lecture 12: Disjoint Sets (Union--Find), Part 2}}
