% ==================================================================
\section{Where We Are}

\begin{keybox}[Recall]
In the \textbf{forest} implementation, \textbf{Find}$(x)$ costs $O(1 + \text{length of the Find path})$ and \textbf{Union} costs $O(1)$. With \textbf{Weighted Union (WU)} every tree has height $\le \log_2 n$, so $\sigma$ costs \textcolor{violetT}{$O(m\log n)$}. We now add a second heuristic to push this down further.
\end{keybox}

% ==================================================================
\section{Heuristic 2: Path Compression (PC)}

\begin{defbox}[PC rule (MUST KNOW)]
During \textbf{Find}$(x)$, after reaching the root, make \textbf{every vertex along the Find path a direct child of the root}.
\end{defbox}

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.85,
  pn/.style={circle,draw=heapblue,line width=0.8pt,minimum size=0.55cm,inner sep=0pt,font=\small\bfseries,text=rustW,fill=white},
  up/.style={heapblue,line width=0.8pt,-{Stealth}}]
% before: chain x->y->z->w->root
\node[pn,draw=rustW] (x) at (0,0) {x};
\node[pn] (y) at (0.9,0.7) {y};
\node[pn] (z) at (1.8,1.4) {z};
\node[pn] (w) at (2.7,2.1) {w};
\node[pn] (r) at (3.6,2.8) {r};
\draw[up] (x)--(y); \draw[up] (y)--(z); \draw[up] (z)--(w); \draw[up] (w)--(r);
\node[font=\small\bfseries] at (0.7,2.9) {Before \textbf{Find}(x)};
% arrow
\draw[-{Stealth},line width=1.2pt,violetT] (4.4,1.4)--(5.6,1.4);
% after: x,y,z,w all children of root
\node[pn,text=black] (R) at (8.6,2.8) {r};
\node[pn,draw=rustW] (X) at (6.8,1.1) {x};
\node[pn] (Y) at (7.8,1.1) {y};
\node[pn] (Z) at (8.8,1.1) {z};
\node[pn] (W) at (9.8,1.1) {w};
\draw[up] (X)--(R); \draw[up] (Y)--(R); \draw[up] (Z)--(R); \draw[up] (W)--(R);
\node[font=\small\bfseries] at (8.2,2.9) {After \textbf{Find}(x)};
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

\begin{keybox}[Why PC helps: amortization]
PC \textcolor{rustW}{increases} the cost of the current \textbf{Find}$(x)$ (it rewires the whole path), but it makes \emph{every future} \textbf{Find} on those nodes cheaper, since they now sit one step below the root.
\[
  \Rightarrow \text{the \textbf{average} (amortized) cost of each Find drops.}
\]
\end{keybox}

% ==================================================================
\section{A Quick Detour: the Tower $2^{*n}$ and $\log^* n$}

\begin{defbox}[$2^{*n}$ (a tower of 2's)]
\[
  2^{*0} = 1, \qquad 2^{*(n+1)} = 2^{\,2^{*n}} \ \ (n \ge 0).
\]
Equivalently $2^{*n}$ is a stack of $n$ twos: $\;2^{2^{\,\cdot^{\,\cdot^{\,2}}}}$.
\end{defbox}

\begin{center}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.3}
\begin{tabular}{l l}
$2^{*0}=1$ & $2^{*3}=2^{2^{*2}}=2^4=16$\\
$2^{*1}=2^{2^{*0}}=2^1=2$ & $2^{*4}=2^{2^{*3}}=2^{16}=65536$\\
$2^{*2}=2^{2^{*1}}=2^2=4$ & $2^{*5}=2^{2^{*4}}=2^{65536}\approx 10^{19729}$ \;(REALLY big)\\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

$2^{*n}$ \textcolor{rustW}{grows astronomically fast}. (For scale, the number of atoms in the observable universe is about $10^{80}$, already dwarfed by $2^{*5}$.) Its inverse grows astronomically \emph{slowly}:

\begin{defbox}[$\log^* n$ (iterated logarithm)]
\[
  \log^* n \;=\; \min\{\, k : 2^{*k} \ge n \,\}.
\]
\end{defbox}

\begin{center}
\small\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4.5pt}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.3}
\begin{tabular}{|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|}
\hline
$n$ & $1$ & $2$ & $3,4$ & $5,\ldots,16$ & $17,\ldots,65536$ & $65537,\ldots,10^{19729}$\\
\hline
$\log^* n$ & $0$ & $1$ & $2$ & $3$ & $4$ & $5$\\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{keybox}[Takeaway]
$\log^* n$ \textcolor{greenO}{grows so slowly} that for any input you will ever meet, $\log^* n \le 5$. It is effectively a small constant, yet formally it still goes to infinity.
\end{keybox}

% ==================================================================
\section{Time Complexity with WU and PC}

\begin{keybox}[Main Theorem (MUST KNOW)]
With \textbf{both} Weighted Union and Path Compression, executing any sequence $\sigma$ of $n-1$ Unions mixed with $m \ge n$ Finds takes
\[
  \textcolor{violetT}{O(m\,\log^* n)} \text{ time.}
\]
That is, every such $\sigma$ takes at most $m\log^* n$ time (for large $m,n$, within a constant factor).
\end{keybox}

Because $\log^* n \le 5$ in practice, this is \textbf{essentially linear} in the number of operations, a huge improvement over $O(m\log n)$ from WU alone.

\begin{warnbox}[Careful with the quantifiers]
The theorem is an \textcolor{greenO}{upper bound}: \emph{every} $\sigma$ costs \textbf{at most} $O(m\log^* n)$. It does \emph{not} claim $O(m)$. Whether some $\sigma$ is forced to cost $\Omega(m\log^* n)$ or more is a separate (lower-bound) question, settled by later work below.
\end{warnbox}

% ==================================================================
\section{Analysis of the Disjoint-Set Forest: a Timeline}

\begin{keybox}[History of the bounds]
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.4}
\begin{tabular}{l l l}
\textbf{Year} & \textbf{Result} & \textbf{Who}\\
\hline
1964 & forest implementation introduced & Galler \& Fischer\\
1973 & $O(m\log^* n)$ upper bound (WU + PC) & Hopcroft \& Ullman\\
1975 & $O(m\,\alpha^{-1}(m,n))$ upper bound & Tarjan\\
1979 & $\Omega(m\,\alpha^{-1}(m,n))$ lower bound (restricted model) & Tarjan\\
1989 & $\Omega(m\,\alpha^{-1}(m,n))$ lower bound (any structure) & Fredman \& Saks\\
\end{tabular}
\end{keybox}

Here $\alpha(m,n)$ is the \textbf{inverse Ackermann function}, which grows even more slowly than $\log^* n$. The 1989 lower bound shows this near-constant per-operation cost is \textcolor{rustW}{optimal}: no disjoint-set data structure can do asymptotically better.

% ==================================================================
\section{Past Exam Questions}

\begin{warnbox}[Terminology bridge for exams]
Exams (and CLRS) use \textbf{union-by-rank} and the operations \texttt{MakeSet}, \texttt{FindSet}, \texttt{Union}. Union-by-rank is the height-based cousin of our size-based WU: both hang the ``lighter'' tree under the ``heavier'' root and keep trees shallow. \texttt{FindSet} with path compression is our \textbf{Find} + PC. \texttt{Rank} starts at $0$ for a singleton; merging two equal-rank roots bumps the surviving root's rank by $1$.
\end{warnbox}

\begin{exambox}[Seen on a past exam \textnormal{(Apr 2022 Final, Q3a: draw the forest)}]
Run the following on a forest (union-by-rank $+$ path compression), initially empty. Write each node's value and its \textbf{rank}. For each \texttt{Union}$(x,y)$ with a rank tie, the new representative is the root of the set containing $x$.
\begin{quote}\ttfamily
MakeSet(5), MakeSet(2), Union(5,2), MakeSet(3), MakeSet(4), Union(3,4),\\
Union(5,3), MakeSet(1), MakeSet(8), Union(1,8), FindSet(8), Union(4,1)
\end{quote}

\begin{answerband}
Walking through it: \texttt{Union(5,2)} ties $\to$ $5$ root (rank $1$), $2$ child. \texttt{Union(3,4)} ties $\to$ $3$ root (rank $1$), $4$ child. \texttt{Union(5,3)} ties $\to$ $5$ root (rank $2$), $3$ hangs under $5$. \texttt{Union(1,8)} ties $\to$ $1$ root (rank $1$), $8$ child. \texttt{FindSet(8)} returns $1$ (path already length $1$). \texttt{Union(4,1)}: root of $4$ is $5$ (rank $2$), root of $1$ is $1$ (rank $1$); lower rank hangs under higher, so $1$ becomes a child of $5$ (rank of $5$ stays $2$).

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.95,
  en/.style={circle,draw=heapblue,line width=0.8pt,minimum size=0.6cm,inner sep=0pt,font=\small\bfseries,text=rustW,fill=white},
  up/.style={heapblue,line width=0.8pt,-{Stealth}}]
\node[en,draw=rustW,line width=1.1pt] (f5) at (2,2.4) {5};
\node[en] (f2) at (0.4,1.2) {2};
\node[en] (f3) at (2,1.2) {3};
\node[en] (f1) at (3.6,1.2) {1};
\node[en] (f4) at (2,0) {4};
\node[en] (f8) at (3.6,0) {8};
\draw[up] (f2)--(f5); \draw[up] (f3)--(f5); \draw[up] (f1)--(f5);
\draw[up] (f4)--(f3); \draw[up] (f8)--(f1);
% ranks
\node[greenO,font=\scriptsize\bfseries] at (2.55,2.65) {r2};
\node[greenO,font=\scriptsize\bfseries] at (-0.15,1.2) {r0};
\node[greenO,font=\scriptsize\bfseries] at (1.5,1.4) {r1};
\node[greenO,font=\scriptsize\bfseries] at (4.15,1.2) {r1};
\node[greenO,font=\scriptsize\bfseries] at (1.5,0.0) {r0};
\node[greenO,font=\scriptsize\bfseries] at (4.15,0.0) {r0};
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}
One tree: root $5$ (rank $2$) with children $2$ (r0), $3$ (r1, child $4$), and $1$ (r1, child $8$).
\end{answerband}
\end{exambox}

\begin{exambox}[Seen on a past exam \textnormal{(Apr 2022 Final, Q3b / Dec 2018 Final, Q2)}]
\textbf{(2022, Q3b)} $L$ is a linked-list disjoint set with head pointers and union-by-weight; $T$ is a tree disjoint set with union-by-rank and path compression. For a sequence $S$, let $T_L$, $T_T$ be the total nodes accessed on $L$ and $T$. Show there is a sequence with $T_T < T_L$.

\textbf{(2018, Q2)} Given a disjoint-set forest with union-by-rank $+$ path compression, \textbf{(a)} draw the tree after a \texttt{FindSet}, and \textbf{(b)} draw the tree after a \texttt{Union} and state its rank.

\begin{answerband}
\textbf{(2022, Q3b) idea.} Build a tall tree, then do many Finds on its deepest node. On $L$ each Find is $O(1)$ (head/back pointer), but on $T$ the \emph{first} Find triggers path compression, after which every later Find on that path is $O(1)$ too, while the list keeps paying for the long concatenations done by its Unions. Pick $S$ as: a run of Unions that makes $L$ redirect many pointers (large $T_L$) but that $T$ handles with $O(1)$ rank-guided Unions and shallow trees. Then $T_T < T_L$.

\smallskip
\textbf{(2018, Q2) method.} \texttt{FindSet}$(x)$: follow parents to the root, then re-point $x$ and every node on the path straight to the root (path compression); ranks are unchanged. \texttt{Union}$(x,y)$: find both roots, hang the smaller-rank root under the larger-rank root; on a tie, pick one root and increase its rank by $1$. The new tree's rank is $\max(\text{rank}_x,\text{rank}_y)$, or that value $+1$ if the two ranks were equal.
\end{answerband}
\end{exambox}

% ==================================================================
% ==================================================================
\section{Midterm-Style Process Problems}

\begin{examplebox}[Predicted: FindSet with path compression]
The forest below was built with union by rank. Run \textsc{FindSet}$(e)$ with \textbf{path compression} and draw the result.

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[every node/.style={circle, draw=heapblue, line width=0.7pt, minimum size=7mm, inner sep=1pt, font=\small, fill=white}]
\node[font=\bfseries\scriptsize, draw=none, fill=none] at (-0.2,0.8) {Before:};
\node[draw=violetT,line width=1pt] (a) at (0.6,0) {a};
\node (b) at (-0.2,-1.1) {b}; \node (c) at (1.4,-1.1) {c};
\node (d) at (-0.2,-2.2) {d}; \node (e) at (-0.2,-3.3) {e};
\draw[-{Stealth},heapblue,line width=0.7pt] (b)--(a);
\draw[-{Stealth},heapblue,line width=0.7pt] (c)--(a);
\draw[-{Stealth},heapblue,line width=0.7pt] (d)--(b);
\draw[-{Stealth},heapblue,line width=0.7pt] (e)--(d);
\draw[-{Stealth}, line width=1.2pt, rustW] (2.6,-1.6) -- (4.2,-1.6);
\node[rustW,font=\scriptsize\bfseries, draw=none, fill=none, align=center] at (3.4,-1.15) {FindSet(e)\\ + compress};
\node[font=\bfseries\scriptsize, draw=none, fill=none] at (5.2,0.8) {After:};
\node[draw=violetT,line width=1pt] (a2) at (6.8,0) {a};
\node (b2) at (5.4,-1.3) {b}; \node (c2) at (6.1,-1.3) {c};
\node[fill=goldpath!25,draw=goldpath] (d2) at (7.5,-1.3) {d};
\node[fill=goldpath!25,draw=goldpath] (e2) at (8.4,-1.3) {e};
\draw[-{Stealth},heapblue,line width=0.7pt] (b2)--(a2);
\draw[-{Stealth},heapblue,line width=0.7pt] (c2)--(a2);
\draw[-{Stealth},goldpath,line width=0.9pt] (d2)--(a2);
\draw[-{Stealth},goldpath,line width=0.9pt] (e2)--(a2);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

\begin{answerband}
\textsc{FindSet}$(e)$ walks $e\to d\to b\to a$ and returns the root $a$. Path compression then re-points \emph{every node visited} straight to $a$, so $\textcolor{goldpath}{e}$ and $\textcolor{goldpath}{d}$ now point directly at $a$ ($b$ already did). Ranks are \textbf{unchanged} (rank only changes inside \textsc{Union}, on a tie). Flattening makes later finds cheaper: this is the amortized payoff behind the $O(m\log^{*}n)$ bound.
\end{answerband}
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}[Predicted: $\log^{*}$ and the sequence cost]
(a) Evaluate $\log^{*} n$ for $n=2,\,4,\,16,\,65536$. (b) State the cost of $m$ operations under weighted union alone, versus weighted union plus path compression.

\begin{answerband}
\textbf{(a)} $\log^{*} n$ is the number of times you apply $\log_2$ to bring $n$ down to $\leq1$:
\[
  \log^{*}2=1,\quad \log^{*}4=2,\quad \log^{*}16=3,\quad \log^{*}65536=4.
\]
It grows absurdly slowly: $\log^{*} n\leq 5$ for every $n$ up to $2^{65536}$, so in practice it is a small constant.

\textbf{(b)} Weighted union alone: a sequence of $m$ operations costs $O(m\log n)$. Adding path compression brings it to $\textcolor{violetT}{O(m\log^{*}n)}$, essentially linear. (The tight bound is $O(m\,\alpha(m,n))$ with the inverse Ackermann function, proved optimal.)
\end{answerband}
\end{examplebox}

\section*{\textcolor{violetT}{Quick Reference: Lecture 12}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Quick Reference: Lecture 12}
\begin{keybox}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.35}
\begin{tabular}{l l}
Path Compression & in Find$(x)$, re-point every node on the path to the root\\
PC effect & costs more now, cheaper later (amortized); average Find drops\\
$2^{*n}$ & tower of $n$ twos; $2^{*5}\approx 10^{19729}$; grows super fast\\
$\log^* n$ & $\min\{k : 2^{*k}\ge n\}$; grows super slowly; $\le 5$ in practice\\
WU alone & $\sigma = O(m\log n)$\\
WU $+$ PC & $\sigma = \textcolor{violetT}{O(m\log^* n)}$ (essentially linear)\\
Tarjan 1975 & $O(m\,\alpha^{-1}(m,n))$, $\alpha$ $=$ inverse Ackermann\\
Fredman--Saks 1989 & $\Omega(m\,\alpha^{-1}(m,n))$ lower bound (optimal)\\
Exam terms & union-by-rank $\approx$ WU; FindSet $+$ PC $=$ Find $+$ PC\\
\end{tabular}
\end{keybox}
